MUNICIPAL ELECTIONS.

CONTESTS IN THREE WARDS AT WARRINGTON.

===== 0002601_19111104_art0012.txt | included=true | included =====
NO CHANGES AND

As a result of the municipal elections the constitution of the Warrington Town Council remains unchanged. The two leading political parties did not challenge the return of any retiring councillor and consequently there were only three contests on Wednesday. at the instance of Independent. Socialist and Labour nominees respectively. Whitecress, Sr. John's and Orford were the wards selected for hostilities, and in each case the expected happened, the retiring repre- sentative having no difficulty in retaining his positron. The most striking fe,ture of the day was Councillor Henry Roberts' record majority cf 603 over Mr. Samuel Gough in Whitecross Ward. Speaking generally, the municipal elections of 1911 will be remembered as the least interestirg that have taken place for many years. There was hardly any approach to excitement, the results being regarded as a foregone conclusion, and the display of mural literature was somewhat meagre. Fortunately for the workers engaged in bringing voters to the poll, November Ist belied its :traditions and for the greater part of the day the weather conditions gave no cause for com- plaint. With the exception of Whitecroes Ward, visits to the scenes of the poll in the earlier part of the day disclosed comparativey little activity in the neighbourhood cf the booths, but in the evening matters took on a brisker aspect and there was a r ush of voters just before the closing of the poll.

===== 0002601_19111104_art0013.txt | included=true | included =====
WHITECROSS-WARD.

Councillor If. ROBERTS, with twenty-one yearn' service in the Council to his credit, was opposed by Mr. S. Gough 'who has long been conspicuous In agitations with reference to town's affairs. Both candidates fought on Independent lines, but Mr. Roberts had a particularly representative platform and a strong ward cunmittee which left no stone unturned to ensure a decisive victory. Mr. Gough did not hold any public meetings, but Mr. Roberts had a very i large and successful gathering on Monday even- ing n St. Barnabas' school. On election day many motor cars were placed at Mr. Roberts' disposal and all day long they were speeding hither and thither conveying electors to the tell. Hundreds of children assembled near the entrance of the b ,oth, wearing Mr. Roberts' favours and cheering every vehicle that appeared on the scene. Mr. Gough appeared to have few workers and fewer, if any, vehicles. In his election literature he made a great point of the living wage question and also expressed himself against increases cf salary to highly paid officials. The electors were asked to r' Vote for Gough and true economy. Then you will have a chance of getting gas, water, and electric light and rower cheaper." Mr. Itnberts also expressed himself a thorough-going supporter of a living wage for workingmen. In a final appeal to the electors, hie committee stated . "No effort should be spared to secure such a majority as will suitably express the opinion of the burgesses in the oppnitlon offered to Mr. Roberts, and on the waste of ratepayers' money which this futile contest has caused. Our candidate is a man of ability and integrity, closely associated with us, and has for many years rendered invaluable services to the town. It is impoitant in the public interest that every elector who believes in the decencies of public life and in honest and capable municipal administration should not fail to record his vote on this occasion for Mr. Henry Roberts." ST. JOHN'S WARD.

The Conservatives were called upon to defend their position in St. John's Ward, Councillor Fred Lashford having a Socialist opponent in the person of Mr. James liempson. Each candidate had appealed to the voters through the medium of public meetirgt, but the general attitude appeared to be one of apathy. However, more interest was manifested on election day aid over 940 votes were recorded. Mr. Lashford had the advantage in the matter of vehicles. A circular issued on behalf of Mr. Lashford stated : " Remember that Socialism and Atheism, which mean the same thing, have done more harm to the workers during the last century than any other agitation ever introduced." The voters were further called upon to" Remember that Socialism means the abolition of religious teaching from your schools." Mr. Lashford also told Lis tonstituenta that be was "the first to propose that an increase be given to the poorer paid workers of the Corporation, and to protest against the raising of the salaries of the alteady well-paid officials." Mr. Hampson, in his address, stated that he would place the health and comfort of the people before pirate profit. He characterized us an outrage the action of the Education Committee in not putting the Act for the feeding of school children into operation because the hungry children were few in number. Mr. Hampson pr mined to advocate an eight hours' work- ing day and a minimum standard rate of sixpence per hour for all Corporation workmen. The cover of his election address was embellished with the declaration:

" The idle tramp and the idle Peer a,e th parasites on industry. The former is sometimes a ratisance, the latter is always s curse." Socialism, it was added, would rovido useful work for al.

===== 0002601_19111104_art0014.txt | included=true | included =====
ORFORD WARD.

Councillor J.Halliwell. the popular Liberal repre- sentative of this ward, was opposed by Mr. George F. Robson, the nominee of the Trades and Labour Council. The Liberal ward committee worked most vigorously on Mr. Barnwell's behalf but the contest was conducted with great friendliness and gcoi feel- ing on b:th sides. Mr. Barnwell had allowable regard for the intereets of the wgkers hv inducing the Town Council to accept a resolution fixing

===== 0002601_19111104_art0015.txt | included=true | included =====
BIG MAJORITIES.

minimum wage of 21s. per week for all able-bcdied employes of the Corporation. This action has commended itself to the ward, which is almost entirely workingolass in its population, and Mr. Halliwell's advocacy of improved tram services for the ward and the abolition of the water rate in respect of baths was welcomed as further evidence of his solicitude for his constituents. Mr. Robson expressed himself in favour of an extension of municipal enterprise. The municipal control of our coal supply alor.e, he said, would mean a direct saving to every householder. He also called attention to the Medical Officer of Health's statement that a large quantity of milk which contained the germs of consumption Was being distributed daily in the town. With this terrible danger in our midst, said Mr. Robson, it rests with the Corporation to establish infant milk depots in order to provide those infants who, for some reason or other, are deprived of the natural food, with pure milk. At a public meeting on Moaday evening Mr. Robson had the support of Mr. J. A. Seleon, ex-11.P. for the Newton di vision.

rfiE RESULTS.

SOCIALIST CANDIDATE'S BITTER OUTBURST.

At eight o'clock the polling booths were closed and the ballot boxes promptly conveyed to the Town Hall. The counting took place in the Council Chamber in the presence of the Mayor and the returning officers for the several wards, viz. : Alderman John Evans (Whitecrcss), Alderman D. Tinnion (St. John's), Alder- W. J. Forshaw (Ortird). Alderman Pierpoint was also present, but by his disqualification as a hurgess, which took effect on November Ist, be was precluded from acting as returning officer for Whitecross Ward, the duties being undertiken by Alderman John Evans. At 8 45 the returning officers announced the results as follows :

MR H. ROBERTS CANDIDATURE.

Reply to Mr. Gough's Circular.

A well-attended meeting in support of the candidature of Councillor 11. Roberts, the retiring conncillor for the Whitacre's Ward, was held in St. Barnabas' schoolroom 03 Mondry evening. Alderman James Evans occupied the chair and there were also present on the platform Councillors H Roberts, Birtles and Hawthorn Messrs J Ssethuist and P Peacock, and Captain d R Croalleld. The CH LIAM AN said that he had never known in all his experience of the Ca oacil a more unnecessary election than that which had been forced upon them in Whitecross Ward. It was the duty and the business of everybody to make the town and place in which they lived" second to none," and be oelieved that it was the aspiration of every member of their Council to make that town as beautiful and as comparable as any in Lancashire. .

Councillor HAWTHORN moved the following resolu- tion : That thin meeting desires t thank Cnuncillor Roberts for his services during th 3 past two years ant pledges itself to use every legitimate effort to secure his election on Wednesday next by a large mnjoritv." They could call this a con- test in which energy bad been unneoessarly used. There had been a great deal of personal enmity and claptrap introduced by Councillor Roberts opponent. He (the speaker) could say that there was really no comparison between the two contestants.Tbe work done by Cancilior Roberta on the Education Committee was sufficient to merit his return to the Council. He hoped that every man who was a Llberal and who had confidence in Councillor Roberts, would assure his return by record- . .

iog all votes in his favour. (Applause). Mr. THOMAS Deus econded the resolution, which was supported by Mr. J. H. SMETHURST and Councillor BIRTLES.

Mr. J. H. SMETHURST said that Councillor Roberts was deserving of their support if only for the honesty of purpose with which he had served them in previous years. He thcught they should have the best men representing them on the Council, and he firmly believed that the time was rapidly approaching when the question of principle would be put before yolitics, as he believed that all should work t3r the interests of the town and not for any particular body. Councillor Itoekturs said that he had always tried to . . . . . . .

the beet of his knowledge to serve the interests of the town generally, during a period extending over 21 years as a member of the Council. As long as it was their desire that he should do what lay in his power to give them the advantage of any knowledge or experience he possessed, subject to the limitations of physical strength, it was his earnest and honest desire to continue in service for his native town. He thought he bad a right to ask why he was being opposed at that election. His opponent had issued a pamphlet in which he stated that it was not his intention to address publio meetings in that ward. He (the speaker) only wished he had favoured the ward with a sample of his porsonalitlei,and what he would any on municiral matters, so that people could see and hear for themselves. Mr. Dough in his circular stated that be (Councillor Roberts) walked out of the Council Clamber and did not support the resolu- tion of Councillor Haßiwa, which was to bring about a living wage for Corporation workmen. Such astute- meat wan a falsehood. He was compelled to leave the Council through an extremely important and private engagement, but if it had been possible for him to have remained at the Council meeting he would not have hesitated to support the resolution. Another statement in Mr. Dough's circular was that the recent purchase of the land is Lovely-laie was ultra tires. That meant contrary to law, but he (the speaker) could tell them that whether it was' ultra vices" or not theConncil had obtained the land and what was more, they had paid for it through the nose as they know they would have to pay for it through the nose. He would do all in his power, if he was returned to the Council, to press upon the Corporation to make such use of the remainder of the ground as would not only provide a playground for the children but a place of recreation where those in the eventide of their lives could go and spend a pleasant evening. With regard to the tenders of the Council for a Duplicate Pumping Engine for Delph-lane Water- works for which Mr. Gough bad stated that was only one firm had got patterns, the speaker asid that he was ins porition to state that there were from PO to 100 firms who were makers of that particular encine, while as to the fact of their being able to save £5OO a year by the use of electric power from the generatin: station to work the engine, the whole cost yearly of the engine and fuel only amounted to £516. The fore- sight of the Council in regard to the water supply had justified itself during the past few weeks. Whereas other towns had been forced to cut the supply short and stop night work, Warrington had not suffered in the least. He thought the Csmacil were entitled to credit, having taken time by the forelock, and had even been able to , produce a profit. Mr Gough asserted that the total indebte loess of the town was £1,500,000, and said that such • position of affairs with a private company would lead to bankruptcy. As a matter of fact, the capi.al out- lay was £1,2,33,000, or £236,000 less than £1,500,000. He supposed that a mere quarter of a million mattered little to nuenciers of Mr Gongh's standing. (Laughter.) But the assets of the town were £1,855,000 giving a surplus of assets of £571,000. Thera was no town in England whose Saar :All position was much stronger, and scores had an inferior position. Financially Warrington's position was extremely sound and satis- factory. Dealing with the question of the feeding of the necessitous school children, he said that he would promise that if the Education Cymmittee were prevented by the Board of Education regulations from seeing that the Act was pat into operation, the winter would not pass without a strong volantsry effort being made to provide the funds and save the town from being disgraced by the knowledge that any child wen going to school in an unfit state throngh want of the roce.rez t.??d, ide,s compelled them to shoulder the respoustbniti. that in estalt OR: the parent could not undertake for the nke of the ohne. He was anxious that a body of representatives of manu- facturers, workpeople and teachers of the town should be formed at the earliest opportunity to deal with this important question, so that there would be prospects of I better t•lture for the children. (Applause.)

TO Grey te THE NEW NORTH—By the com- pletion by the Canadian Northern Railway of a spur of KO miles of line connecting Edmonton with.l Athahaace Landing on the Athabasca River, a farther important step has been taken in opening up the great region of the McKenzie basin to trade and commerce The line will be in operation early in November. From the Landing communication by steamer can be main- tained to the mouth of the McKenzie River, 2,000 miles distant to the north, one interruption only to navigation, by rapids, occurring above Fort McMurray. From the Landing also a service of steamers will be available for convex ing settlers and their effects to the western end of the Lesser Slave Lake, from which xriat the fertile Peace River country is only 40 mike distant, and where also the last great area of free land in Canada is situated. The construction of this rail- way comes at an opportune time, for the North-West Mounted Police report the existence of much suffering due to the shortage of food, among the large both of pioneers who have teen pressing into the Peace River Country.

INDEPENDENT METHODISTS -

Mrs. John Crosfield op2ns New Sunday School.

SPEECH BY MR. A. H. CROSFIELD.

Saturday was a day of rejoicing at Fri.,' Green Independent Sfethodist Chu-ci, for it witnessed the opening of the n,e/e Sunday school, the provision of which is necessitated by tile ever-expanding uork of the inrituti in. The ceremony _was performed by

Mrs. John Crosfield, of Colwyn Bay, in the presence of a large and represeutatite gathering. The new school, which occupies a site on the north eide of the chapel, has been di signed with a view ultimately to forming part of a comprehensive scheme for replacing the existing church hall with a new pet of buildings, of which the school will form a wing. Admirably adapted for its purpose front every point of view, the school is 43ft by 42k in dimensions, the main entrance being m Cairo-street, through a glass vestibule. The floor is of wood blocks, hid on a good concrete foundation. A rJstram and reading desk have been erected against the west wall. The work has been admirably carried cut by Messrs. JJseph Dolan and Sons, builders, Warrington, to the designs of Mr. S. P. Silcock, F.R.1.8.A. . . . .

Amongst those present at Saturday's interesting proceedings were : The Mel or and Mayoress (Conn- . _ . _ _

;illor Dr. and MiSFI Joseph),Mrs. John C'oneld, Mr. A. H. Crosfield, Alderman and Mrs. Forahaw, Al leg- man Bennett, Alderman Jan.es Evans. Councillor and Mrs. Benin°. Couccillor Henshall, Mr. and Mrs. S. P.

Silc -ck. The congregation flint assembled in the chapel and joined in singing the hymn All people that on earth do dwell." Prayer wan offered by Councillor Itenehell. A move having been made to the entrance of the new school, Mr. S P BILCOCK, the architect, ssid he hei been sated by the I riendo to present Mrs Croslield with a gold key as a memento of the ceremony. He was sure that if they bed looked the whole of tVvrington over.

thry could not have selected a more kind and generom lady to open the school. (Applause,) It was four months since the foundation stones were tail and that afternoon's ceremony was a most suitable ant appropriate finish to the undertaking. The erection of the tuilding had not hero accomplished without a greet amount of thought. He bad erected a considerable number of buildings in his time but he had seldom had to deal with a piece of land like that which the new school stord upon. None of its bides were equal . . .

and tone of its aiagles were right angles. (Laughter.) Mr Si!cock concluded hy thanking the Building Com- mittee for their support. He had also been well backgl up by the builder,Sir Dolan, who had done his work in a creditable and workmanlike manner. (Applause.) Mrs. CROSFIELD briefly returned thanks for the key which she said could always remind her of that pleas- ing and interesting ocoasion. She declared the school open in the name of the Father and of the Son, and of the Holy Ghost. Mrs. Crosfleld having openei the door, a meet log was held in the new building, piceided over by Alderman James Evans. Mrs. CROSFI 6LD said it had given her great pleasure to come there to help all those throngs whoae salt- denying efforts that beautiful Sunday school had been built. She had often heard of Friars' Green, but that was the first occasion she had been inside the chapel. A few weeks ago, when their good friend Alderman Evans came over to see her, she was very much interested in what he told her about that wonderful man Peter Phillips—(applause)—who a hundred years ago used to walk miles and miles in all kinds of weather, over rough moors and over Chatmoss to officiate at numerous little chapels in the dstrict. His seal and earnest faith must have inspired and animated the congreation to build that new school. They were not a wealthy body and they must have had to face rather large financial difficulties. A DEADLY PARALLEL

CHILDREN'S CORNER, Conducted by UNCLE CALEB tz...---•ww-N..—w-N.,---•.—.:...:

DO HENS TALK ?

Grandma, do you s'pose hens can talk? ' asked Alice. "Old Speckle talked to me the other day," said grandmother. "I mean she made me understand what she wanted." "Why, grandma! How did she do it r And Alice's eyes grew big and round. "It was when Mr. Gray was making the new fence. I heard a noise at the kitchen door, and when I opened it there was Speckle, bristling her feathers and cackling away at a great rate. She turned at once and went round the house as though she knew I would follow." "Oh, grandma!". interrupted Alice, "Did you ?" "Of course," said her grandmother. "She went as fast as her hen legs could carry her down to the fence, and when I got there I found two of her little chickens had fallen into a post-hole while Mr. Gray had gone to his dinner."

"Wasn't it nice that you found them?' said Alice, with a sigh of satisfaction. "I do b'lieve hens can talk, if they can make people understand them so well."

"There's the rooster coming into the yard," said grandmother. "He looks as though he has something on his mind. Let's watch him and see if he says how-dy- do."

It certainly looked like it. The old fellow stalked up to the hens, who stood very still and seemed to be listening with great atten- tion. In a minute he turned round and went out into the lane, both hens following him. "Oh, Grandma," said Alice. "he did say something, I know he did! I'm going to see if I can find out what he told them."

"I'll go too," said grandmother, as she put down the peas. Alice ran ahead. "Come quick ! Come quick ! " she called, as she looked into the field on the other side of the lane. "They're having a party? ' Sure enough, they were having a feast of worms. It was plain to be seen that the rooster had found a quantity of them where the ground had been freshly plowed, and had told the hens about it.l

THE POPPY LAND EXPRESS. The first train leaves at 6 P.M. For the land where the poppy blows, The mother dear is the engineer, And the passenger laughs and crows. The place-car is the mother's sties; The whistle a low, sweet strain. The passenger winks, and nods, and Llids. And goes to sleep on the train. At 8 P.M. the neat train starts For the poppy land afar. The summons clear falls on the ear, "All aboard for the sleeping-car !" But "What is the fare to poppy land? I hope it is not too ' dear The fare is this - a hog and a kiss. And it's paid to the engineer. So I ask of Him who children took On his knee in kindness great; "Take charge, I pray, of the trains each day That leave at 6 and S. Keep watch of the passengers," thus I pray, " For to me they are very dear; And special ward, 0 gracious Lord, O'er the gentle engineer."

-EIXAR W. ADD 7T THIS WEEK'S STORY.

"Now, Nora," said the departing physician to the Irish girl, who was nursing a bad case of fever, "if the patient sees snakes again, give him a dose of this medicine. I will be in at six."

The hour for his return arrived. The physician once more visited the sick patient, and found him raving. He had been so, said the nurse, for hours. "And did you give him the medicine?" inquired the puzzled doctor. Nora shook her head.

"But didn't I tell you to give it to him if be saw snakes again?" demanded the physician. "But he didn't say he saw snakes this toime, doctor," replied the nurse, confidently. "He said he saw red white-and-blue turkeys wid straw hats on!"

COMMERCIAL FAlLURES.—According to " Kemp's Mercantile Gazette," the number of Commercial Failures published In Great Britain and Ireland during four weeks ending Saturday, October 20th, was 697. The number in the corresponding four weeks of last year wen also 697, showing no alteration, being a net decrease in 1911, to date, of 613.

MORE JOKES ABOUT THE WEATHER

An American tourist came across a man out West sitting on a stump. " How's the weather treating you ?" he asked. " Pretty tolerable, stranger," replied the man. "I had some trees to cut down, but a cyclone came along and levelled them for me."

"That wa3 a piece of luck," cried the tourist.

" Yes ; and then" continued the man, "there was a storm, and the lightning set fire to the brushwood and saved the the trouble of burning it." . .

" Remarkable ! But what are you doing now ? "

"Oh, I'm just waiting for an earthquake to come along and shake the potatoes out of the ground." Strand Magazine.

THE DEFAMATION OF THE TERRITORIAL FORCE.

Misuse of Lord Kitchener's Name.

The following Imter which has appesnd in several of the leading daily newspapers, throws an interesting light on the controversial methods of the advocates of compulsofy military training. Admiral Fitzgerald, it will be rememlrred, recent'y addressed a National Service League meeting in the Parr Ball, Warrington. Sir.—The following appeared in the " Daily Mail " for Friday, October `2oth, and I understand, in many other newspapers throughout the country:—

===== 0002601_19111104_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
LbaD KITCIIENER'S RE r6RT.

Admiral Penrose Fitzgerald, addressing a meet- ing in connection with the Notional Service League at Liverpool yesterday, said Lord Kitchener and Lord Haldane were present at certain Territorial manoeuvre,. At the close Lord Kitchener offered acme criticism. "Yea," said Lord Haldane, " but they have got enthusiasm." "Srhas a mob," replied Lord Kitchener. As a Territorial officer I am one of those who are engaged in the uphill work of persuading ths young men of the country that, whatever may be the desira- bility or the reverse of IMMO system of compulsory service, it is the present duty of the individual in the absence of compulsion to qualify himself to serve his country in arms by joining the territorial Force and submitting vo;untarily to the discipline and training which thus lie, as it were, at his own front door. Our recrui!ing difficulties would, however, ba still further increased if it were believed that Lord Kitchener had expressed any opinion adve, s• to the force, and, above all, it he were thought to view it as n 3 beAer for military purp3ses than a mob. Obviously it wm'd be unreasonable to ask a young min to sac ifice leisure and convenience for work that so high a military authority considered to be usdees. I theref re wrote to the Admiral asking him whether he was correctly reported, an 1, if so, what was his authority for the story. I have received from him the following reply: " 1, T inky Crescent, Folk estone, October 26th, 1911. " dear sir,—The enclosed is a practically correct report of what I mid !at Liverpool. I took the story from a pwagraph in Nome paper some little time ago' and I regret that my memory does not enable me to say what paper it woe. I merely mention this to show that it was not a mere srnok'ng-r mm yarn' thlt I ,was repeating.—l ate, yours faithfully,

C. C. P. FITZGERALD."

By the same post I have received a let.er from the S:cretary of State for War, in which he authorises me to say that " the tale is without f undation. . . . Lord Kitchener never made any such remark to me, nor was I even pre eat is his company at lerritorial maw:micros." As already st dad, our main difficulty in finding recruits for the Territori d Force lies in persuading them that the sacrifice is worth while. This being the case, may I plead with you t 3 give publicity to this by no means isolatei instance of the methods of the National Service League ? If the Leapt) and their members must rr.ist in the strange perversion that they are doing patriotic work by decrying those who are serving voluntarily because they want every- one to be compelled to do so, may I at least suggest that they ought in common fairness to ve,ify their facts before they quote high military authority to popular audienoili in support of their views ? Yours Ac ROBERT A. JOHNSON. 15, Tedworth Square, L uidon, S.W., October 30th.

THE LATE MR, HUBERT CONGREVE.

Memorial Service at Liverpool.

Many friends of the late Mr. Hubert Congreve were present at an impressive memorial service which was held at the Church of Humanity, Liverpool, on Sunday evening. The representatives of the Ails Canal Compsny present included Captain Waring;Latchford.) ailemn and beautiful music was rendered, including Beethoven's Funeral Mach, Mozsit's " Larghetto ' quintett from and Handel's Dead March is " SauL" The service was conducted by Mr. Sydney Style a member of the legal profession—acting pustor of the Church, who after giving an interesting sketch of Mr. Congreve's career said: Hubert Cingreve was the living embodiment of the noblest qualities of man- hood. Truth was his lode star. Dtry was the dominating fact a- of his life. He was intolerant of injustice. Ile was merciful to the weak. Conscious of his own ability, he deprecated praise and abhorred flattery. His o,urtesy to all was proverbial. He was prodigal in his benevolence. His life was a distinctly religious life, but a religious life guided by two forms of faith separated by a period of transition. His mother was a member of the evangelical section of the Church of England, and at King's College Dr. Barry and Archdeacon Watkins influenced him in the same direction. He became one of a band of young men who went into the slums of London to teach the Gospel of Christ. When working at the Tay viaduct he was a constant attendant at the Scotch Episcopalian Church and at the time of his engagement he was anxious that his future wife should be inwardly convinced that her soul was saved. But as active duties occupied his time, as he became more widely read in poetry, history and phibsophy, as his love of pictures and church srchitecture increased be gradually grew out of the evangelical creed and became conscious of the truth and beauty of the teaching of hie uncle, Richard Congreve, and the meaning of his life, and sobs adopted an attitude of sympathy to our church, and later, when he came to know Albert Crompton, joined it as his own. He was greatly impressed with Albert Crompton's memorial address en his uncle, Dr Richard Congreve, and came to see how the faith of Humanity, as contained in the writings of Auguste Comte, was able to inspire and guide the life of a practical man. His interest in the cause here was wry deep. In his last letter to me he txpreseed his interest in our proposed new church and suggested the style of architecture fitting for its construction. In that Church we may hope he will long be held in remembrance, that many generations of practical men may be iuspired to incorporate his ife in theirs.

IRISH AFFAIRS. -- NOTES FOR IRISH READERS. - -

THE INSURANCE BILL AND THE NECESSITIES OF IRELAND.

The desire of the Government to have the Insurance Bill passed into law is abundantly clear from the allocation of time for Parlia- mentary business. The proceedings of the Government in this regard, it will be remembered, was embodied in a resolution which was carried by a majority fully repre- senting the preponderance of the Progressive forces in this Parliament. That the measure is sound in principle, that its object is a desirable one is common ground as between all parties in the House. But—and this is our point on this important current topic—so far as Ireland is concerned in the scheme it has been shewn from the moment of the introduction of the Insurance Bill up to the present time that in order to make it suitable to the needs of that country the measure must be radically altered. The " caveat " entered by Mr. Redmond on the night of the introduction of the Bill has been approved in the popular discussion throughout all Ireland which he invited. The special needs of the sister isle ought surely to be known to the Government. Not for the first time English statesmen have learnt that a reform fraught with great benefits to the people of England and Scotland, might, if unamended, prove oppressive in Ireland. In the case of the Insurance Bill, the danger was probably greater than ever. Asidrafted, it is now agreed, it contained provisions which would have weighed heavily on certain British interests. If this was true of a measure drawn solely, and after long study, with regard to the welfare of the working population in Great Britain, it was safe to conclude without any examination of details that the peculiar necessities of Ireland were practically ignored in the scheme. Consider- ation of the provisions of the Bill has proved the soundness of this view. Mr. Redmond and his colleagues at once declared that in order to make it applicable to Ireland the measure required "serious amendment." No other course was open to them if they were to be mindful of the needs of the people who send them to Westminster. And their opinion was universally endorsed in Ireland. There were no two opinions in Ireland on the necessity for considerable amendment, and it was evident that if the Bill was to be made suitable to Ireland it would have to be, in its Irish portion at any rate, largely recast. Now the Chancellor of the Exchequer in deference to British interests, has promised many amendments of his Bill though it was plainly thought out beforehand and framed with a view to meeting the peculiar needs of Great Britain. Anyone can see with half an eye that these alterations are by no means unimportant. In some respects they are fundamental. Mr. Lloyd George has made important concessions to powerful organisations in England and Scotland. The Irish demand is that he should consider the peculiar wants of a country whose industries and social condition differ RO vastly from those of Great Britain. And so keenly is this felt that for the first time in history the reverend prelates who form the heirarchy of the Catholic Church in Ireland, who are free to look at the question impartially and solely with regard to the interests of their flocks, have passed a resolution on a matter clearly of an economic as distinct from a religious character. It may be worth while to emphasise here the fact that on this question the attitude of the Irish Party have been perfectly consistent. The amend- ments proposed by the party are in accord with the suggestion of the Bishops of Ireland. Naturally we are hopeful that these amendments will be accepted by the Government,which has accepted amendments from the Front Opposition Bench, and we think, as supporters of this Government we can await discussion on the Irish portion, Clause 59, without anxiety. A LIBEL ON MR. REDMOND.

A political libel case not without signifi- cance for Liberal and Tory alike, was settled in court the other day, when Mr. Basil Peto, M.P., sued a Mr. Beesley in the following circumstances. The day before the poll at Mr. Peto's election a leaflet was issued on behalf of Mr. Peto in which the well known apocryphal passage from a speech by Mr. John Redmond was set out,in which he was falsely declared to have stated that there was not an Irishman in America that would not rejoice to see a German Army march from Yarmouth to Milford Haven. Mr. Redmond as we have pointed out in this column before has repeatedly repudiated this speech. Mr. . . . .

Peto, however, shewed he had no pre-know- ledge of its employment for him. Mr. Alfred Beesley wrote to a local paper saying he had got Mr. Redmond's denial and proceeded to criticise the use of this fabricated passage especially on the eve of the poll. It now turns out that it was supplied by a Mr. Hardy who teaches the young idea at Marlborough College, who took it from the" Daily Mail." It was admitted that Mr. Redmond had never made the statement, but the extra- ordinary feature of the case is that Mr. Peto claims exemplary damages from Mr. Beesley for the suggestion that it was kno x- ingly used. Thus the Tory candidate on whose behalf the false statement was issued becomes the injured party and secures £350 damages at the hands of a London special jury 1 What we should like to know is— where does Mr. Redmond come in? Suppose he took an action against every Tory candi- date or speaker and every" high-toned" Tory newspaper by which this fabricated passage has been used, where would it end? It might easily go on to the close of the twentieth century and even then not touch all the fabricators !

simple. CATCH-MY-PAL On Wednesday evening, in the Parr Hall, the Rev. R. J. Patterson, L L 8., had an extraordinary story to relate of the " Catch-My-Pal" movement, of which he is the author. While yet this latest success—the most remarkable since the elsys of Father Matthew— was in its infancy, I had the facts from the lips of • personal friend of Mr. Patterson having a ministerial charge in Mancoester. The idea was a splendid one, I thought then ; anyhow, it has since justified itself abundantly. What progress has been made in this as in other respects since the days of which we have heard with our ears and our fathers have told us ! We have often read how, in the time of Charles 11, it was considered correct for every 'gentleman and lady of fashion to drink more than was advisable of Canary and buttered ale and hippocras. Just as the consump- tion is less to-day, the number of sarieties he also decreased. An Elizabethan writer men- tions 56 varieties, while a still older poet

gives a catalogue Of medireval wines, the i;iere reproduction of which would require too much of my rapidly vanishing srace. To take a leap forward, the " seven men of Preston " signed tile pledge on September let, 1832, says a correspondent. The word " teetotal " was not accidentally invented by Richard Turner. Dr. F. It. Lees shows quite clearly that the word was in common use in Lancashire and Ireland and had been used by De Quincey and Denim, the Irish novelist, long before Turner was born. To suppose that Turner originated the word while " stuttering " Dr. Lee deecribes as an err( r. By-the-bye, I once had a psiticulsrly note- worthy experierce with Dr. Lees, but I must reserve that story for the present. Ihe first temperance pledge was an anti-spirit pledge, which allowed the members to drink malt liquor in moderation. This was improved upon on September Ist, 1832, when the pledge" rim all liquors of an iroox'cating quality" was signed by Joseph Livesay and six others. For a considerable period both these pledges were in existence, and it was at a meeting in the Preston Cockpit in September 1833, that Turner, in a fervid speech, declared for the pledge against all intoxicating liquor, They must have done with it all and be "teetotal." Mr. Livesey, who was in the chair, cried out, 'That shall be the name," and the word teetotal was thus applied to the total abstinence movement. As the "Daily News," to whose columns I am indebted for the ability to present these interesting facto in this bandy form, showed in the course of comments upon the annual report of the Commissioners of Customs and Excise, the nation is conquering its appetite for liquor. Undoubtedly, Mr. Patterson will rank high among contemporaries who have materially belprd their weaker fellows onward and upward in this regard.

JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

BENEATH THE BED. The only bit of Byron's "Don Juan" that has my case stuck through the years is the one telling how in the course of a memorable hunt "they looked beneath the bed, and there they found—no matter I what, but 'twas not that they songht." Poor Mrs I Chrimes, the ged licensee of the Roebuck Hotel, did the like last Saturday night, and discovered an alleged burglar, who, after fiercely (smiting her, disabled himself in attempting to escape. It is said the land- lady made a prar tire of looking under the bed when retiring for the night. As I read of this I was reminded of the nervous married woman who likewise made this an invariable rule, and on one occasion noticed a pair of boots sticking out. With a terrified shriek she aroused her husband, who had already dropped off. "John, John," she cried, 'there's a man under the bed ! " "I'm glad you've found him at last," was his cool response; 'you're been looking for him 20 years to my knowledge! " PELORUS JACK. Some time ago I confessed to a weakness I r listening to the harangues of market quack doctors and the patter of gutter merchants. Latterly I have been vastly entertained by the methods and humorous asides of a bumble female vendor of cheap curtains, and recently missing her from her accustomed weekly stand, I was quite shocked to think that I should ne'er fee nor hear her more. Happily, as in the case of the late Mark Twain some years ago, the rumour proved to have been " greatly exaggerated." And so, I I rejoice to find it is with the world-renowned "Felony' Jack." I am very glad indeed of ID. Fred Stott's assurance that I was mistaken in supposing that that old and most interesting Dolphin was dead; and if I , owe " Uncle Caleb" an apology—well here it is it all 'sincerity and heartiness, along with my compliments. "Jack's alive," as a once popular parlour pastime bath it, and I for one, found the news such good read- ing that I can climb down more gracefully than would in some other circumstacces be possible. MEAN THEFTS. The conviction of two unspeakable wretches at the Borough Court of despicable thefts from poor children goes to show to what depths some cowardly criminals ' will descend for the sake of quite paltry some, Years ago I had the pleasure of witnessing the committal of one such who had made a speciality of robbing girls on their way home from work. It woe in a Lancashire cotton district, and the victims were mostly weavers living in remote parts. They were attacked on pay nights under cover of darkness, the thief throwing his overcast on the girl's head and then ripping out her pocket. Once an intended victim bad taken the pre- caution to carry her purse elsewhere, and the fellow was foiled in his attempt, but the shock seriously affected the weaver's health. Finally, the heartless ' rogue met his match. Ile set upon a lady teacher who resided with her sister at a lonely farm, and she resisted to such purpose that he was fain to flee, leaving in her hand his cap. This led to his undoing, for a local constable identified it and promptly arrested the owner. I may add that these things happened wirhin a mile or two of the birth-place of the Rev. H. Ogden, whine formal recognition es pastor of Golborne-street Baptist Church took place amid general felicitations on Thureday evening. KING DAU %ID AN' HIS KIST 0' WHUSTLES. The promised re appearance of Mr. David Clegg, who is advertised to give an organ recital at Bold. street Wesleyan Church this evening, is well calcu- lated to awaken memories and to induce reminiscences. I think there is a sense in which it may without offence be suggested that this amazing organist has always b 'en more or leas in the habit of" tickling the ears of the groundhogs " Perhaps I had better make my point clear by means of an illustration." There is no doubt about it," said Mr. Noter, "be is a cultivated , musician, " And what is your idea of a cultivated musician ? " asked his friend. " One who plaj things you don't care to hear, and talks about them in • way you can't understand." What I mean is that Mr. Clegg would not dream of putting before a mixed audience things they would not care to hesr. It behoves me to be extremely careful here, because I mind me of a time when, well into the teens of years ego, I criticised one of Mr. Clegg's performances at a certain Wesleyan chapel, and so "put my foot in it" as to cause deep and lasting resentment. Writing of the popular player's happy knack of never-failing appeal to the general, I wrote by way of convenient summary, "The common people hear him gladly." I should not like to have it supposed that my Methodist readers did not recog- nize the quotation. They Feemed to suspect an implication which was never intended, and, with lees

material than some weedd have deemed nrceasury for a molehill, proceeded laboriously to rear a muntain which is doubtless there onto this day. Apps.-ently the humour of the situation did not strike anyono but that ribald newspaper fellow. Seriously, let me add that in the meantime I have received from Mr. Cl !gg at leaat one frankly appreciative letter acknowledging my press notices of his work. OUR GREATEST ACTRESS.

In fulfilment of a fairly long standing engagement with the Philomathic Society, the still incomparably charming, surprisingly vivacious Miss Ellen Terry is due at the Parr Hall next Thursday. night. An unoccupied Feat on such an occasion were indeed difficult to imagine—as difficult as it is for the beholder to realise that this, the first actress of the day, years ago celebrated her jubilee on the stage! I enlarged somewhat on this fruitful th,,me the other , week, and may return to it hereafter. As another enthusiastic admirer expresses it, Ellen Terry is the most marvellous alchemist of our time ; she has dis- covered the Elixir—the secret of perpetual youth, and she is willing to share the recipe with all. A curious questiorer once assed," Why do you look so young?' And she replied, "I think it is because I am never long unhappy ; I always try to be happy.' Her parents figured on the boards ' • her brother Fred and her sisters Kate and Marion achieved distinction before the footlights; and her son Gordon. if not conspicuous as an actor, is well known on the Continent as well as in England as a man r,f ideas, "particularly in regard to novel methods of staging and lighting." I note that Miss Terry is to deal here with " some of the heroines of Shakes- peare's plays, with illustrative acting." It is an exceedingly wise choice. It is her pet subject, and she is sure to be at home in it. When only eight years of age she played Maximillius in ""f he Winter's Tale," and actually had in her andieacethe late Queen Victoria and the Prince Consort—and a favourable notice in the" Times " on the following day. AB a girl she played nith Mrs. Kendal (I moan the two romped together as young companions). She ale° appeared with her in Mr. Tree's revival of " The Merry Wives of Windsor." Sa long ago as 1863, when the present Bath Theatre was opened with a production of the" Midsummer Night's Dream," Miss Terry playd Titania and Mrs. Kendal " second sing- ing fairy." I have not yet seen her Warrington programme, but I dare say it will include her mistress Page, Lady Macbeth, Portia, Beatrice, and Ophelia ; for in these, as in others not a few, she is truly wonderful. BOROUGH FREEMAN.

So Mr. Robert Pierpoint is to be added to the Free- men of the Borough of Warrington. What does such honour imply and involve? I have a hazy sort of recollection that I have at least touched upon this question before, but at such a juncture it agitates the minds of many, and, in view of the strange attitudes assumed by one and another in relation to it, the well- ml among my readers will pardon my dwelling upon it furtherior the sake of those who confessedly know little about it. If any student of life among Ithem happened occasionally to drop into the taproom of a Warrington" pub" he would in all probability there find some alleged " authority" solemnly assert- ing that those upon whom the honour in Question is conferred are fortunate enough to escape the payment of all rates, taxes du. Others say that they are entitled to a certain sum yearly out of the rates. Andl thire are yet others who maintain that Freemen are privileged to frequent theatres, concerts, and other similar functions free of charge—that they are, as the honour implies, literally Freemen whom Dame FoAtine has singled out to enj y a life without taxes, with free entertainment, and a veto in the borough I although they may not reside there or have property. I Upholders of each of these ideas are to be mit with ' in places where men do congregate, and many a time and oft of late has argument waxed hot an to which held the right opinion on the subject. There are many prepared to back their own pet theory with large wagers. It is therefore as necessary as it is timely once more to explain that a Freeman of the B Tough of Warrington receives no special privilege whatever. It is simrly an honour. At one time, Lot very remote, Freemen of certain boroughs njoyed certain privileges. They varied of course. In York, for instance, there are at the present time, quite • large namber of Freemen who receive what is known as an emolument out of the rates. I may rematk in passing that I happen to be well acquainted with the Clerk to the City Council there. When I first knew him, he was in training in the office of an uncle, who is himself a magistrates' clerk and an active member of the Manchester Literary Club. To proceed, how- ever, there are others who are granted pseture land free for their cattle. But an Act of Parliament passed in 1885 swept away all this, and now, when any borough desires to confer such honour upon any of its citizens, it means nothing more than an honour to

Warrington Socialist Society.

The first of the weekly winter meetings of the above society wee held in their large room, Wildempool Sunday evening, M. James idampeon presiding. Mr. Jack Dort of St. 'relent', was the speaker, the enblect being Pauperism and its Cure," fie contended that to deal effectively with any evil it was necessary to understand its origin. In • very interesting way he traced the commencement of pauperism in Eng- land, attributing it mainly to the stealing of the common lands from the people. From 179! to 1816. the " patriotic 'landlords enclosed two million acres of the people's land, which bad br.en common property ifor at least a thou.and years. This while the males ' were fighting for the nation in foreign lands, sad when the soldiers returned from war—into which they had been preered—they were deprived of their livelihood. the agricultural lands having been transformed into pasture lands etc. During the last :At years • further seven million acres have been filched from the people. Dealing with the evil of pauperism Mr. Burt mentioned incidentally the Minority Report of the last Poor Law Commission. Although a palliative there was traceable in its composition • more humane method of dealing with the question than bad hitherto been deemed necessary. In conclusion the speaker con- tended that landlordism end capitalism were the root causes and that they must be abolished and replaced by public ownership The only permanent care there- fore for pauperism was Socialism. Several questions were asked at the close.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

Taxing tho Unmarried.—The princildo of taxing bachelors has been already adopted in this country by the grant of special income-tax exemptions to married men with families, but one of the smaller German I'. incipalities—that of Reuss—hes distinguished itself I by imposing a super-tax on spinsters as well as bachelors. The matter is regarded as of some import- ' ance in the Fatherland, as it is probable that a super- tax on unmarried persons will he adopted in the larger States, if it successful in Reuss. There is little doubt that the bachelor has to look forward to the time when, if be will not have a wife and family of his own, he will be made to contribute more than he does to the relief of those of other people, but it is hardly fair to put the same burdens upon spinsters, who are debarred by the conventions of society from making a proposal of marriage. In Reuss the super-tax is to apply equally to bachelors and spinsters over thirty, and whore 'memos exceed £l5O a year, but equalisation of this kind must sooner or later mean equalisation all round, and an end to the passive attitude which social conven- tions impoas upon women in courtships. The Oddities of Life.—One cannot help smiling at the oddities of life, of which an example was afforded by the man who appeared before a London magistrate I with a crying baby in his arms, and asked for " maintenance order, or something of that sort," against his wife. He wan compelled i y law to support his wife, who continually went away and left him with the baby, so that he was hindered from doing his day's work. The magistrate was sympathetic but said he could do nothing for him as" the law Ie all in favour of the wire." In another case, the magistrate raid it was time women should be obliged to take out licences for hatpins, which were quite as dangerous in the hands of violent women as a revolver is the hands of a man. That they are a danger to the general public when used only for ordinary purposes, has been proved on many occasions by accidents caused by projecting DOints. Even the feminine bat itself may be a source of peril, for it wag recently reported that a green bet worn by a lady, seeing a friend off at a railway station was mistaken by the engine driver for the waving green light of the guard, and In started the train before the proper eignal was given. The Soap Making Industry.—Some interesting parti- culars concerning the soap industry appear in a November Mag .zine from which it appears that we are one of the best washed nations in the world. The avenge consumption of soap in this oountry is about fourteen pounds per head per annum, whilst in Russia it is only one and e half pounds. Whether we always enjoyed this pre-eminence may be doubted, for in the time of James 1., the industry was the exclusive mono- poly of the Company of Soapmakers of Westminster, who had to pay the King a duty of .ti 6 per ton, on all soap produced. There are several reigning monarchs of the present day, including the German Emperor, who are engaged in business, but there is nothing new in royalty embarking in enterprise of this sort. James I. pushed this particular trade by a proclamation which declared the company's soap to be "good, sweet, and serviceable for our people." Of all commodities soap has been one of the most widely advertised, and an English firm went far to capture the American trade by securing a testimonial from Henry Ward Beecher, which began by saying "Cleanliness is next to Godliness." The arm who bought Sir John Millais's picture "Bubbles" for purposes of artistic advertising, is said to have disbursed altogether something like £3,000,000 on proclaiming the merits of its goods. Biblical Quotations.—" Cleanliness is next to godli- ness" is commonly supposed to be a Biblical rr quota- tion" hut it is not to be found In the Bible. The mistake, something like the latest" hall " which Mr. Birrell bestowed on the House of Commons when, in answer to a question on Irish affairs, he referred to "a wholly garbled version of what never took place." Thst is dintinctly Hibernian, and has someolaim to rank with the famous comment that " half the lies which are told about Ireland are not true." Another saying which is oftea wrongly supposed to be in the Bible is "God tempers the wind to the shorn lamb." The meaning of the reference in correct enough, but the simile is a little queer, because as a rule the lamb is not shorn. The saying " Cleanliness is next to godliness " is sometimes attributed to John Wesley, but it was only 0.5 a quota- IT that he ueed it in some of his sermons. Embroideries and Contrasts.—The vroolivork embellishment, so popular this winter for dreams, and _millinery, requires skilful handling, if it Is not to savour of the grotesque ; of the beauty and suitability of the _carpet embroderies however, used as revers es some of the new and more expensive coats, there can bone two opinions. Eastern in design, 'lob in colour, the reds, blues, yellows, and green of the ancients predominating, these gorgeous garnitures of velvet) softnees, offer a strong and striking contrast to the 110111br0 shades of the coats they adorn. Contrasts are a decided fete.ure of this meson's fashions. Take for instance the shade of grey, which is the colour of the moment, and note how beautifully it Is brought oat whoa contrssted with with royal pnrple, the russet tints, or deep orange. Belts again, of black patent leather are used with afternoon dresses of the brightest tints. Sometimes narrow bands, not more than one and a half inches in width, consist of no fewer than three colours, deftly folded in this narrow space, and inch is a contrast with the shade of the dress with which this novelty is worn. Satin belts are also seen, and on them tiny white buttons are arranged to form • design lithe back, which ialauppoeed to add grace to the

figure. Among accessories, the fichu comes flrst.and its variety is endless. A novelty is of black net, and this is draped over bodices of all colours. The fiche of the same material as the dress, in scalloped, and bound to correspond with the flat mid scalloped flounces no often placed on afternoon frocks. A Cosy Wrap.—An evening wrap that should com- mend itself to the practical woman with a limited allowance, le of reversible blanketing similar to that used for infants' cots. It in very file and soft in make, and the coloure are charming, and any two shades may be chosen to snit the wearer individually. The edges of these wraps are neither turned up nor hemmed, but finished with wools of the same shades as the material, in blenket stitch. The only expensive part of this I novel end effective wrnp, is the corded trimming which finishes the hood, but even this may be dispensed with, I and a suitable ribbon in colours to match and of a striking design, be substitntel. Such a wrap presents no difficulties to the home dressmsker,and well rewards the time and trouble spent on it. About Forw—fit would seem that fur is cultic iently of the nature of an od.rrnment by itself, nod that to garnish it with trimmings is totally unnecessary. Designers however hold other opinione, and heavy ornaments, braid, fringe, velvets, chiffons, and lane are all pressed into the service of paltry with varying success. The combining too, of one pelt with another, goes merrily on—take for instance mole with ermine. A few years ago no furrier would have need the two together, yet they make a vivid contrast and are so skilfully and deftly arranged that each seems to display the other to the best possible advantage. Fine silk fringes and chenille fringes on furs are very popular, the former in conjunction with very r'ide braids. Grey , chenille fringes with grey furs and white silk and , chenille fringe with ermine, are distinctly successful. ' Grey seminal is among the prime favourites this ' winter. The fashion for grey furs has brought grey wolf to the fore. it is both a good-looking and a well. wearing fur, and these merits probably account for its popularity. Black wolf seems to have taken the place of black fox, the price of which is prohibitive to all but the very rich.

The monthly meeting of this Council was held at the Council Offices on Wednesday afternoon, presided over by Mr J Backhousa. _ The CLERK stated that he had received a reply from the Chief Constable of Preston, with regard to the appointing of an Inspector at Sankey to issue 'Mentes for the removal of pip, stating that the matter should receive attention. POLILTON-NT ITIL•FEARNII IA D. NEW SEWAGE SCHEME ADOPTED.

The SURVEYOR submitted ta—the-&-taz-ciran entirely new scheme for the disposal of the sewage from Poulton-with-Fearnhead. Ile said that the last scheme was prepared in 1905 since which time there had been many changes. In the first case the original treatment site proposed had been built upon and rendered unsuit- able and secondly importabt and improved methods bad been found which materially altered the ideas regarding the treatment to be adopted for Poalton, at the same time making it possible for a gravitation system. Ills proposals were to gravitate the whole of the township sewerage to the south end of the field. adjoining Paddington hock, that being the only point where the township possessed a direct river outran. From there he would in commend the carrying of a 21 inch pipe touching the east aide of Padgate brook and striking Padgate-lane, at a point opposite the "Mount," continuing the 21 inch sewer along the south footpath to the junction of Tidy-lane, then a 21 inch pipe passing under the level crossing through the fields to the l'ost Office. Ile would continue that size to Fearnhead Cross with a 15 inch pipe some 400 yards up Cinnamon-lane reducing with a birch pipe to Blaokbrook and continuing with a Sin. pipe to Cinnamon-brow. The levels he had chosen for the main could collect sewage from part of Blackbrook-lane, hearnhead-lane to about the hind Brooch /teen., the whole of the village and also the length from Padgate Bridge to the " Mount." Ile also recommended that an additional sewer should be laid to the annul castledl across the fields to the " Grange," but that it should not be taken into this main. The railway company would have to be approached with regard to the enlverting at Tidy-lane crossing. He estimated the coat of the piping as £4,611 is, which did net include the treatment system which would probably cost a further £2,000. The scheme was adopted by the Council on the proposition of Er. MURRAY seconded by Mr. W., Mums.

LIQUOR IN NEW ZEALAND.

The Dominion's Chance to " Co Dry."

In accordance with the Licensing Act passed by the New Zealand Parliament last year, the coming elections in New Zealand will be accompanied by a poll on the liquor question which might result in the Dominion as a whole declaring for prohibition. The Licensing Act of 1910 was a great advance on the local option system which had been in use since early in the nineties. Under the old system, districts were enabled to decide triennially whether they would have no change in the number of licences, rrduction, or abolition. The 1910 Act cuts out the reduction isene, retains the other legal option provisions, and adds means for establishing prohibition throughout the Dominion. Local prohibi- tion will be voted for on separate ballot papers, and in each case a three-fifths majority will be needed to make the change. Under local prohibition all bars I in " dry " districts have been closed, but residents in them have been legally entitled to have as much liquor as they please in their haulm. Under Dominion prohibition, ouches may be now established, it will be contrary to law to " import, make, have, or sell" alcoholic liquor except for scientific or medical purposes. An examination of the results of local option polls: in the pest shows that the "no licence " policy has been steadily gaining. Twelve out of a total number of 76 districts have decided on " LO licence." In 1968 the six districts previously under " no licence " voted against restoration of licences, six new districts came into the " dry " area, seven carried reduction, and no fewer than 38 had a plain majority for "no licence," but not the necessary three-fifths. Altogether 182 licences have been abolished, reducing the pro- I portion from one licence for every 34.4 persons to one for every 721.

PASSED.

Robertaffutrodoced hie Territorial Army Amendment Bill. (Applause.) Captain Roberts then dealt with the proposals of the Bill that each divilioo should be com- manded by • regular general offiaer' and each brigade by • regulsr offioer not below the rank of Colonel and that all comb Want officers should be reqroi ed to attend • class of instruction in musketry at Brim. With regard to drills, it was proposed that recruits should have to put in at leaet nO instead of 10, during their flast year, and as that worked out at only a little over one drill per week, it was not much to expect of n roan who was going to be paid for attend- ing a certain proportion of them. The drills for trained men would be 40, es against 10 at present, and those were few enough to keep them trained in the use of their rifles and for the purp-ose of discipline. All ranks would have to attend the annual training for the full period of 15 days and the experi. ence of the local battalion showed that that could easily be done. When the Werrington battalion formed pert of the field army brigade in the old volunteer days, from 1900 until the year before the Territorial scheme came into force, they took as an average 99 per cent, of their total strength to camp for the whole fifteen dam The Bill further provided that separation %How- ere, should be paid to nil N.C.O.'s and men attending camp. Another innovation was the payment for drills at the rate of two drills equal one day's army pay without allowances. To-day the officers and men got absolutely nothing for the work they put in voluntarily. They had no encourage- meet, exsept the privilege of wearing a red coat at ceremonial parades. Ifs Territorial Army was required, he maintained that the cost-try ought to pay tor it. (Hear, hear) Under the Bill they did not pay for the whole of the work but only for • proportion. Person- ally he would liko to see all the drills paid for on that basis. A proposal which they believed would make for the efficiency of the force was the allocation of one sergeent-inetructor to each company. Adjutants would also have a greater inducement to raise the discipline, morale and general tone of their respective unita by the payment of a bonus, equal to Cr') a year. Hullo ranges would be provided within reason- able distance of the headquarter, of every infantry regiment. At present the Warrington battalion had to go to Altar to flee a musketry course once a year and the only additional practice provided was on the miniature range at the Drill Hall. That, he con- tended, was not generally sufficient to mote good marksmen. Finally the Bill enacted that permanent summer camps should be organised where men could fire and drill, on full pay, on Sat urdajs and Sundays; at least one of these week-end camps to be undertaken before the annual training. Dealing with the question of coat. the War Mi later said Imperial Parliament voted £2,660,000 for the year 1910-11 for the Territorial Forceand that included £28,000 for the Channel Islands and Col inial Militia. That worked out at £i 00,000 for each of the II divisions, Toe additional three divisions on that basis would cost an extra sum of .070,000, and the pay suggested would bring the further expenditure up to nbout £1,000,000. If the replirements of the Bill were carried out the Force would be able to take the field within a few weeks of the liesile of mobiliza- tion orders, with a fair chance of putting up a decent show against any armv that might land on these shores. To-dav the Territorial Force was not what it ought to be. That "zooid not be expected, with the present inadequate training and comparatively small number of drills The Bill was designs i to remedy the present deficiencies' and he hoped the House would pass it unnnimonsly. NOTES BY " MUST."

What the Colonials did on Wednesday, Warrington are expected to do to-day, Le., win at Swinton ' As about twenty of the Warrington players witnessed the math they should have picked up a point or two which might be useful to-day. Australasia won by 28 points to 9, which is rather a big feat for Warring- ton to emulate, but they should certainly be equal to beating the Blues by a fair margin. Last year, War- rington beat the Blue. at Warrington by a try to nil, hut lost at Swintos point, to nil. They are playing better football to-day, and with the Blues handicapped by injuries to Dai Davies and D. B. Davies there should be no doubt about the result.

I hope Warrington will not make the mistake of Thinking they can win the match when they want. The policy should be to put "all in" right from the kick-off, and thus secure a nvirgin in the first half. lithe weather keeps fine then Warrington will profit by playing the open passing game. They have strength forward and pace behind, and if the b3ll is only flung about in the style of the Colonials, points will come. By the way. Creevey might very well try the Colonial method of running round the pack with the apparent intention of getting the centre and wing going, and then pass inside t a forward Ike Thomas or Arnold who is good in the loose. The t,ick needs to be arranged beforehand to be effective. At'Xivat rind Cann, or Courtney, play it to rare purpose, and only last Saturday at Warrington Jones and Roman showed that they had picked up the move. In fact it came off so well that Warrington were more than lucky to escape having a try recorded under their posts at s critical period in the game. Creevey is a'' brainy " player and the idea should appeal to him.

The Warrington team will be stronger to day than it was last week, and the return of Brookes should make a lot of difference. Then, too, Dickenson has been brought back to the threequarter line in place of Unsworth, who has never played a decent game since the opening match of the season. George is admittedly slow, but he is safe and sure on defence, and from the available material there is no better eboice. Bradshaw has been promoted to the forwards in place of Skelhorue, while Brocklehnrst is still on the sick list.

Team : Tilley, full back Waywell, Braejahaw, Dickenfon and Woodburn threequarters ; Brookes and Creevey, half backs ; Thomas, Johnston, Shugars Chestere, Arooll and Bradshaw, forwards.

Last Saturday's match at Warrington was fall of -thrills, for though Warrington finished op with a comfortable margin of 16-7. they were not really safe till the whistle sounded. Rochdale played a robust and clever game—in fact they played better fcotball than Warrington —but the bulk of the scoring was on the Fide of the lccsla who made up for lack of com- bination by dash, and took every oppotrunity that came. The meat remarkable feature of the game was the way in which the five Rochdale forwards beat the Warrington six for possession in the pack. What with poor heeling out on the Warrington side, and Daintith fumbling at the bare of the pack, Creevey was practically a passenger. I never saw a stand-off half get the ball so seldom, and yet Warrington scored four tries to one. Jones and Baxter played a great game for the losers, and the three-quarters must have got through .more than once but for the pace of men like Waywel!, Bradshaw and Woodburn.

The effect of having three speedy men in the Warringlon three-quarter line was noticeable through- out, and thougb the combination was poor, the men were much more dangerous than in most of the previous games this season. Bradshaw has come on in great style, and bids fair to make a better centre than wing. He runs well, handles well, and tackiest well. I wish him luck. Woodburn played a better game, and showed a capacity for beating his man and making play that has been lacking in his earlier displays Waywell was capital, and his pace and pluck were often in evidetce even after be had been badly shaken in ecoring a dashing try at the corner.

Warrington v. Rochdale Hornets.

The Wirepullers recorded a smart victory on Satur- day at the expense of Rochdale Hornets, though such capable players ao Brooks and Taylor were unable to appear through injuries. Rochdale had a strong team and played a keen game, but found the home defence too strong. The weather was One and a crowd of five thousand assembled, the teams and officials being :- 1 Warrington—Tilley, bock; Way well, Bradshaw, Unsworth, Woodburn, three-quarter backs ; Creevey laud Dainteth, half backs ; Johnston, Thomas, Shtig, tin, Arnold, Cheaters and Skelhorn, forwards. Rochdale hornets—English, back ; Ward, Robinson, 'Baxter and Padden, three-quorter backs ; Schofield and Jones, half backs ; Romans, Fitzsimmons, Dearden, Jenkios, Barton and Woode, forwards. Referee, Mr W W Taylor, Hunslet ; touch judges, Messrs A Cross, St. Helens, and H Heath, Runcorn. Rochdale opened in strong fashion, and good work by both forwards and backs gave the home defence an anxious time. Bradshaw and Waywell were both cheered for fine tackling, but several decisions by the referee did not find favour with the crowd. Warrington had to fall back as the result of penalties, but when Tilley dropped a floe god l from near halfway the cheering broke out afresh. Warrington's front rank proved very aggressive in the loose, and English, the Rochdale custodian, was severely hurt in stopping a rush and took no further part in the game. Baxter took up the rear position and made some fine clearances. Jones and Robinson made a raid into the Warrington quarters and Tilley was hard pressed, but the ball was worked back cleverly by Bradshaw and Creavey. Woodburn got away on the right and punted across the goal. Cheaters followed up at a big pace, and knocking the ball out of Robinson's bands, dribbled over near the posts for Thomas to add a goal Rochdale were not disheartened and their forwards, showing superior scrimmaging powers, soon had the game in the vicinity of the railway goal. Warrington were bard pressed for a lengthy period, and any attempts to clear were well met by Baxter and Robineon. From a penalty granted near the quarter flag Padden placed a goal. This success gave the Rochdale men encourage- ment, and the forwards forcing matters splendidly, Woods got over in splendid fashion. Paddon placing the sides on level terms when he landed the goal. Warrington took the game to beyond the centre fl eg, but were unable to pees Baxter and half-time came with each side having recorded 2 goals and I try, 7 points. The resumption of play saw the home forwards in a very energetic mo d. Twice Shugars burst through in the Rochdale quarter, and Baxter had once to kick out of play to save. Ward relieved with a dribble but Woodburn cut in on the right and then sent the ball along to Bradshaw who ran strongly before passing to Waywell, the latter avoiding Paddon and scoring cleverly at the corner. Thomas made a poor shot at goal from the difficult angle. The Rochdale forwards responded finely, and clever play by Jones, Romans and Robinson saw filley beaten, but Waywell rushed up and saved at the expense of a bad shaking. Jones was very aggressive behind the active Rochdale forwards, and Daintith nearly gave a try away under the posts. Tilley clearing with difficulty. Then Thomas, Johnston and Creevey all had a hand in a movement which led up to Cheaters rushing over for his second try. The issue was now safe, and though Rochdale made sp ismodio bursts they were always held in check. In the last few minutes Bradshaw effected a fine dribble from centre, and punting past Baxter, he raced clear for a grand try, which WU not improved open. Result : Warrington,two goals and four tries (16 points) ; Rochdale Hornets, two goals and one try (7 points).

LANCASHIRE COMBINATION.

WARRINGTON' A" BRATEN AT ROCRDAT.E.—The " A " team took a weakened side to Rochdale, and were narrowly beaten at the Athletic grounds after a good game. The opening play went in favour of Rochdale, and following good forward work Taylor opened the scoring with an unconverted try. The " Wircpullera " retaliated in effective fashion and after the backs had combined well Povey and Clare scored, Jordan convert- ing the second one. At half-time the visitors led by one goal two tries (eight point") to Rochdale one try (three pointe). In the second half the home aide were more aggressive and Taylor and Gibson notched tries, but in each case the goal kick was a failure. In the closing steam; Jones landed a penalty goal for Roch- dale, who won by 11 points to S points.

THE CHAMPION BAND.

MR A. H. CROSFIELD ENTERTAINS CRYSTAL PALACE VICTORS.

Mrs. John Crostield's Congratulations.

Mr. A. 11. Cro.field entertained the members of the Perfection Soap Works band at the Chapel House Int , Great Sankey, on Saturday, in celebration of their magnificent victory at the Crystal Palace. From the earliest days of the band Mr. Crosfield has taken a keen interest in its rapid rise to national fame, and he was one of the first to extend congratulations on the crowning achievement of a lustrous record of successes. He also expressed a desire to meet the champions at a social function in honour of their Humph and on Saturday they spent a highly enjoyable time as Mr. Crosfield's guests. In the afternoon a bowling main was held, a series of keenly-contested games resulting in the first prize going to J. Fairhurst. Afterwards ea excellent tea was served, and the cele- brations wound up with a smoking concert. Mr. J. Willett presided and sac supported by Mr. A. H. Crosfield, Mr. W. Halliwell (conductor) and Mr. J. Taylor (secretary). During the evening Mrs. John Crosfield, of Colwyn Bay, looked in to congratulate the band and had a remarkably enthusiastic reception

The tendency to" ease off "a little bit most be guarded against, for it RBI the little bit that made all the difference. There was plenty of room for improvement, even in the best of binds, for they bad not by any msans exhausted the possibilities of development in braes baud music. (Applause). _ . - .

A SPECIMEN OF A SKELETON of primitive reptile of about 60 feet in length has been discovered in the valley of the Red Deer River in Alberta by the expedi- tion from the American Museum of Natural History, New York, which is at present working in Western Canada collecting fossil remain. of ancient fauna on the North American Continent. There explorations have been made under tbe authority granted by the Government some eight years age. This find by the American paleontologista has suggested that some effort should be made to secure specimens for Canadian museums. It is believed that there is in the Red Deer country at least one more skeleton of. giant dinosaur and attempts have been made to save it for Cassis.

CROSS-COUNTRY RUNNING.

With the exception of Warrington A.C. all the clubs appear to be rapidly getting into their stride end perhaps the locals are cue of the very few teams whose seasoned runners are following the useful method at this period of the season. Probably a desire to get fit slowly, with a view to undergoing a second and more searching separation for I the strenuous struggle for places on the United King- ' dom team for the Cross Country race in the Orympic games at Stockholm in July next, may account for the unusual tactics followed by so many " old hands " at preseat. Well the chances are that some will, aud a great many will not, be suited by this method, particu- larly if the selectors insist on a series of trial mean to aid their discrimination. .

Sutton Harriers journeyed to the headquirters of Skerton Hurlers and a race over an eight miles trail resulted in a very easy win for Sutton as follows : Sutton : .1 Smart, I; S Welding, 2; D Brown, 3 ; E Harrison, 4; R Smith, 5; J Morris, 6 ; 21 points.
