THE EXAMINER.

SAMRDAY, NOVEMBER 4, 1911

The discussion that UNEMPLOYMENT has raged around the INSURANCE. provisions of the

-ts National Insurance Bill dealing with insurance against sickness on the part of the individual, has left untouched an equally important part of the Bill which deals with the disease of the State, Unemployment is a morbid condition of the body politic, and as such cries out for treat- ment, not only in the interests of those persons immediately affected, but in the interests of the community. For it is not merely an economic disease, but'an infectious economic disease, and that of the most quickly spreading kind. The symptoms, the effects of unemployment, with • which we are all familiar, lose through that very familiarity some of their significance. It i a a matter of common observation that good years and bad years affect all trades alike. It is not a common expedience for one trade or industry to prosper while others languish. Once the evil of unemployment gets a grip on any single trade, and forthwith, as we have seen in many a year of depression in the immediate past, it spreads rapidly through- out the industrial community. That it is infectious being granted, the reason is not far to seek. It is, in a word that the industrial worker is the largest employer in this country. Out of work ho leaves the market as a consumer, and those who are engaged from day to day in furnish- ing him with the necessaries " Whoso has sixpence is monarch to the extent of six- pence; over all men he commands cooks to feed him, philosophers to teach him, kings to mount guard over him, to the extent of sixpence." But if the sixpence be lacking,then cook, philosopher, and king are unemployed to the extent of sixpence. That is the whole secret of the spread of unemployment from one trade to the next. In thus tracing the sdurce of the evil, it is not impossible to lay our hands upon the cure. That evil must be circumscribed, confined to the trade in which it originated, isolated, and destroyed is obvious. That the matter is urgent,Mansion House Funds, panic in legislation in the near past, have rendered clear. Surely here is the cure. If the unemployed person can only be kept in the market as a consumer, though only to the extent of a few shillings.the men whom he indirectly employs, whose products he consumes, suffer the less from his misfor- tune. Give:him but seven shillings in his pocket and he can employ the boot-maker, though his clothier must wait for better trade. Though the philosopher suffers tempo- rarily from unemployment, he can still command the cook to feed him to the extent of seven shill ings. The Unemployment pro- visions of the National Insurance Bill have been criticised as providing merely relief from the distress following upon unemployment, a condition which seems to be regarded on all hands as inevitable. No less inevitable than our grandfathers thought consumption. They are, in fact, provisions not only for relieving, but for the cure of unemployment: provi- sions therefore of the utmost consequence to the State. Turn to the details of the measure. The part that has met with most adverse criticism is the provision for contribution by the employer. The worker who has realised, or who is coming to realise, that he is to receive for 21/2d. benefits that would cost him some- thing over 6d., is not generally hostile to the measure. Complaints come from another source. Employers, who, to do them justice, have accepted readily their liability to contribute under Part 1., are sometimes found complaining of the contribution to be levied from them under the Unemployment Section of the Bill. ,Yet, morally, claim upon the employer in respect of unemployment is even greater than it admittedly exists in respect of sickness. In times of declining prosperity, he must for his own protection discharge hands; as trade .improves again, he is anxious to take them on. It is a matter of direct financial interest to an employer that there should be, outside his factory gate men standing in the market to be hired The reserve of surplus labour exists

===== 0002601_19111104_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
for his prcfit and prosperity, and it is just and necessary that he should contribute to the reserve pay of the workers who are temporarily withdrawn from active service, as indeed, as things now stand, he has to pay sooner or later. The burden of unemployment falls in the end on him, when the first sufferer has dropped by distress to seek shelter in the casual ward. All that the Bill requires of him is that he should discount his liability, that he should make provision in advance, not in times of distress and peril, but in times of prosperity, when it is possible to get the machinery into working order to co-ordinate the interests of masters and men, so that when the lean years come they may stand together beneath the load and neither be overwhelmed.

has placed the people of Warrington by his fine educational work on politics, through the medium of speeches and letters.

Mr. Cr3sfield has MR. CROSFIELD AND always been a Home HOME RULE. Ruler of the most con- vinced and unswerving type. To-day he is, if possible, more enthusiastic than ever for Ireland's just and righteous cause. On Monday evening he took a suitable opportunity of warning the Liberals of Warrington against the "diatribes and terrible prophecies" which would soon be scattered broad- cast through the constituency by • the other side. However reluctant our Tory ' friends may be, they will have to once more make a bogey out of Home Rule, or else have very little to bait the Liberals with. Mr. Harold Smith, M.P., who has not yet favoured us with any of the reasoned argu- ments which he may have against Home Rule for Ireland. has shown himself heartily in favour of the bogey policy. Less than a month ago he told the electors of Warrington that:

Seldom have the THE hixtmoNs. municipal elections in Warrington aroused less excitement or interest in the town than they have this week. Only in three of the nine wards was there a contest, and in none of the three were the candidates fight- ing a political battle. The fact that the retiring Councillors were sure of a triumphant return made even these contests somewhat tame, and it only remains to congratulate Councillors Roberts, Halliwell and Lashford on the size of their majorities. One thing we do disapprove of, and that is the arrangement between the Liberal and Tory parties not to oppose each other in any of the wards. If this meant that the political influence in municipal affairs was being lessened it would be a matter for congratulation, but unfortu- nately the opposite is the case. By a compromise such as this the party political organisations practically ensure that there shall be neither contests in the Wards nor changes in the personnel of the Council. Either way the arrangement—as has been proved by local history in the past—does not conduce to the best municipal government. The parties should either take no concern with municipal elections, or fight one another fairly and honourably for that political dominion at the Town Hall on which so much store is set. Compromises are an abomina- tion, and if misused, a distinct evil!

- " If they thlust upon rater a m•asure of Home Rule the streets of Belfsst and Londonderry would run blood, and the constituents of Lis native city would cross the channet by thousands to take up arms "etc. In Mr. Harold Smith's view Ulster is Ire- land, and Sir Edward Carson its King, while " F.E." is doubtless heir to the throne. We cannot imagine that the people of Warrington will be favourably impress^d with such jaundiced and unpatriotic sentiments from the mouth of their member. The Home Rule question is one for calm, reasonable consideration, and the politcians who deliberately invest it with racial and religious animosities are unfit to represent or advise any body of English electors whether in Warrington or Whitechapel. As Mr. Crosfield says, these tactics may have answered the purpose twenty years ago, but they will not do to-day. His argumentative case for Home Rule and reasonable devolution should be read by every elector who has any appreciation for the responsibilities of the position he holds as a voter, and any concern for the solidarity of an empire which is less united at home than it is abroad.

Mr. Lloyd George's A GREAT reply to the Welsh

The many friends and A COMPLIMENT FOR admirers of Mr. A. H. MR. CROSFIELD. Crosfield will appreciate the compliment paid him by !' Mayfair," the well-known London society journal, in including him in its gallery ' of " Men of the Time." The interesting biography published in this week's issue of the paper refers in the highest terms to Mr. Crosfield's abilities as a politician, business man and sportsman, while a clever and characteristic cartoon depicts him in golfing attire, with club in hand. Mr. Crosfield was busy in the constituency last week end, -fulfilling a typical round of engagements which gave striking proof of the many-sided- ness of his interest in the old town. It was particularly appropriate that he should attend the opening by Mrs. John Crosfield, of Colwyn Bay, of the handsome new Sunday school at Friars' Green for, as was pointed out by more than one speaker, he was thus follow- ing in the footsteps of his late revered father who delighted to encourage religious and philanthropic work. In the evening Mr. Crosfield entertained the members of the Perfection Soap Works band at Sankey, in celebration of their great triumph in the recent national contest. From the very inception of the band, Mr. Crosfield has -followed its fortunes with the closest interest and an ever-increasing pride, as it has mounted the rungs of the ladder leading to its present position of pre-eminence. After addressing words of congratulation and encouragement to the champions, he returned to Warrington to present the bowling prizes at a smoking concert in connection with -the Prince of Wales' Lodge of Oddfellows. Mr. Crosfield had a rousing reception from his fellow-members and the fact that he was able to address them not only as a politician but as a brother of the Order, gave an added significance to his 'brief but none the less important speech. Mr. Crosfield is a whole-hearted supporter of Mr. Lloyd George's scheme of national insurance, being firmly convinced that it will not only confer immeasurable blessings upon" the toiling multitudes but that it will strengthen the position and increase the scope for usefulness of our great friendly societies. Monday evening saw Mr. Crosfield at the Liberal Club where, as is usual at these social gatherings which so pleasantly blend music and politics, he was in his happiest vein. Mr. W. Bibby fittingly voiced the debt under which Mr. Crosfield

CAMPAIGN deputation, on Tues- AGAINST DISEASE. day, besides entering into the special ques- tion raised, furnished a clear-cut exposition of what the Insurance Bill does for the campaign against the "white plague." The Bill, as widened in Committee, not only undertakes to deal with the treatment of consumption, but with the discovery of means for its extinction. Not only the insured person, but hie wife and children will get the benefits of the Bill. Towards capital expenditure the Government finds one and a half millions. Towards maintenance, the insurance fund will find about a million a I year, of which the State supplies something like a quarter. The inclusion of wives and children will perhaps double !the estimated I amount required. The State asks, not by compulsion but by invitation, for the local authorities to provide one-half of the addi- tional cost out of the rates. The other half is furnished by the State itself. So bold, so far-seeing a plan to grapple with one of the greatest curses of civilisation had, we may safely say, never been dreamed of before. The Tories can only reply with the extra- ordinary suggestion to the workingmen, "If you bad let us tax your bread and butter, we might have thought of this, or even something better."

• • Thursday next is what is popularly known as "Mayor's Day" and civic bodies through- out the country will be engaged in electing their chief magistrates for the coming year. Councillor Dr. Joseph will be re-elected Mayor of Warrington at a meeting of the Council to be held at 12 noon • •

As a result of the elevation of Mr. Ernmott to the peerage, the House of Lords will now have among its members three former occupants of the Chair of the House of Commons. The other two are, of course. Viscount Peel, who was Speaker from 1884 till 1895, and Lord Courtney of Penwith,l who was Chairman of Ways and Means and Deputy Speaker from 1886 till 1892.

Following the election of Mayor on Thurs- day next, a special meeting of the Warrington Town Council will be held at 12.30 for the purpose of conferring the freedom of the borough upon Mr. Robert Pierpoint. The order for the casket has been placed but it is not expected that the work will be com- pleted in time for the ceremony. The scroll bearing the resolution of the Council will, however, be, presented to Mr. Pierpoint by the Mayor.

* w It will come as a surprise to many people to hear on the authority of the Postmaster General that the use of the letters "N. 8. as the postal designation of Scotland has no official sanction. In reply to a question in the House of Commons, Mr. Samuel said that as no one now spoke of Scotland as North Britain, and as letters addressed in these initials were liable to be sent to New Brunswick—(laughter)—the Post Office would prefer that they should not be used. * * *

An unpleasant featur-e of the South African War was the stealing of old Dutch Bibles and ether precious family heirlooms from the Boer farmhouses. Many of these spoils were brought to Australia by members of Colonial- contingents. An old Dutch Bible recently came into the possession of Mr. E. W. Cole, the proprietor of Melbourne's famous Book Arcade. He did not put it up for sale, but sent it to General Botha, and the General has just restored the old family Bible to the 'rightful owners. _

The experiment of walking to the polls in an election had its first trial at the Oldham municipal elections on Wednesday. As compared with last year there is a slight fall in the number of recorded votes. In the nine wards last November there were 17,641 votes given. This week the number was 16,629, a diminution of 1,012, which is rather under 6 per cent, of a decrease. If this 6 per cent represents the value in votes of vehicles to carry voters they do not seem to be quite worth while. * *

Workmen have been busily engaged at Warrington Bridge this week making pre- parations for the erection of the temporary wooden footbridge which will relieve the congestion of traffic whilst the actual bridge wokrs are in progress. A portion of the wall on the east side of the bridge has been demolished and cantilevers thrown out from the arch to support the wooden structure. More piles will shortly be driven into the river bed. Mr. Webster, the engineer, and Mr. Thorne,the contractor, have been on the scene this week to decide several points of importance.

The Warrington Assessment Committee was again asked yesterday to pity the sorrows of the poor brewer whose trade is declining through the more temperate habits of the people. According to the representa- tive of Messrs. Greenall, Whitley and Co. the consumption of beer must have decreased by at least 10 per cent, in the last five years —welcome news to the temperance reformer! The committee, however, stood by the figures of the valuer, who had already granted the firm a reduction on last year's valuation, and declined to give the brewers the more sub- stantial relief which they pleaded for. They would be wise to leave well alone!

It is quite likely that, after all the work in committee upon it, Lord Wolmer's Asylums Bill will not get through the House. The reason (says the London correspondent of the "Manchester Guardian) " is interesting and worth considering. Sir Frederick Banbury I has, I am 'told, intimated his determination to object to the Bill, and, of course if he does it has small chance, for a private member's bill cannot be given debating time at this stage of the session. Sir Frederick Banbury has no special hostility to this particular bill—indeed, his objections are usually on some larger ground than the mere bill con- cerned—but is going to oppose its passage because he does not approve of the social reform programme of the young bloods of his party, does not think that Tories are properly occupied in evolving schemes of social reform, and accuses those who are so occupied of meddling with Socialism. This will be worth remembering when the schemes come to be

THE EMPTY RESERVOIR AT APPLETON, where employees of the Corporation Water Department are busily engaged in cleaning the muddy bed for the first time in sixty years. The mud is being turned into the brook which flows down to the Walton Pool, and will eventually be removed from there. This is an easier method than raising and removing the mud in bulk from the reservoir itself.

Brighouse, the districtooroner ttenlel at the Police Station on Friday to log lire into the death of COatlefl Bebbington, aged nearly 12 plars, son of George Bebbington, no iroa works labourer, who made* at 313 Winwick-road ! he was knocked dston and killed by a motor in Winwick-road on the lot inst. It appears that at 6 p.m. on the day in question the deceased and his brother Wilfrid aged 16, ani a boy nursed Hogan were playing near Long-lane about 200 yards from the Borcugh boundary. Wilfrid was running across the road followed by the deceased and the other lad. When Wilfrid reached the opposite side ha beard a mot or approaching from the direction of Winwick. He shouted at the baps and then went back and pushed Hogan on to the footpath. He then caught hold of his brother's hand, when the car struck the witness and curried him about 6 yards, at the same time knocking his brother down. Wilfrid whistled to the motor, which did not stop and then went towards his brother who had been taken about 20 yards. No ha n was blown. The boy was found to be dead and was con- veyed home. The Chief Constable of Warringtoo., and the County police had been actively engaged in trying to find the driver but until now they had been unable to find any trace of him. Under those circumstances it was absolutely necessary that an adjournment should be made ao that the driver should be able to give an account of the matter, as it would be injudicious and not judicial to make any comment until that evidence was forthcoming. C trroborative evidence was given by the deceased's brother Wilfred, who said that he also received injuries to his left leg, for which he had to be treated at the Infirmary. The motor wts of a dark colour, and was covered over. When he picked his brother up bis face was covered with blood.—An adjournment was mode until the 21st inst.

GARNETr referred to the far ward novement in cannec ion wig' the local branch of the British Women's Temperance Assoziati Jll which had resulted jr. 3)) new members being obt titled in one year. Gift to St. Paul's Church.

St. Paul's Church Magazine mentions the fact th a gentleman who recently came to live in the parisht , asked the Vicar and his warden to let him know some- thing of the church work, and the outcome was a gift of .CZO to be used principally to aid the clergy fund, but also to prevent appeals for other organizations which constantly require money. There would be far less bitter feeling against the no-called' rich " (says the Magazine) if more of thin spirit was manifested.

A Curious Epitaph. The Psrisls Church Megazine 'nye : We are greatly indebted to Mr. Robert Pierpoint for a new tombstone commemorating the burial of Elizsbeth Robinson, whose epitaph has interested numberless visitors to the Parish Church. The old stone has well-nigh perished, but enough remained to rsoder legible the immortal words which ars now inscribed afresh:—

"This maid no elegance of form possessed ; No earthly love defiled her sacred breast, Hence free she lived from the deceiver Man, Heaven meant it as a blessing she was plain."

‘' Churches Like Greengrocers' Shops '

Commenting on the recent harvest festival, St. Jame Latchford, parish magazine says :—We are indebted, as usual, to the ladies who gave so freely of their time to decorate the churches (St. James' and St. Hi'date), both of which were suitably though not lavishly adorned with flowers and' kindly fruits of the earth." Harvest decorations have, in many places, been greatly over- done. Churches have been made to look—and smell— like greengroce-s' shops, and heaps of decaying vegetables have been allowed to remain in them for the best part of a week. Excess in decorations is greatly to be deplored and it should always be borne in mind that the Ecclesiastical Festivals, such as Easter and Christmas, are infinitely more important than the Harvest and that the best decorations should be used for the most important days.

An:Example to Churchmen

One of Warrington's most promising young men, aho met with such an untimely and sad end, has (rays "St. Paul's Parish libigszice ") set a worthy example to all churchmen and churchwomen in Warrington, by leaving to the Church in which he loved to worship the sum of £1'1:1 for the upkeep of the same. How few think of this now-a-days ? Good churchmen who die worth much mere than our deceased brother never think of the church tley worship in, in relation to its finances. If, for instance, all those who have died at St. Paul's had only remembered in their wills in a small degree the church they professed to love so much, how much easier it would be to carry on the spiritual work. We cannot undo the past, but we can avoid mistakes in the future ; if all the present worshipFers who are In a position to do Bo would just leave a small sum for the upkeep of the church, their names would be honoured and revered, and it would be an evidence of practical religion.

A sale of work in connection with St. James Church, Latchford, was hell in the schoolroom cm Thursday afternoon, Mr. J. Littler, of Runcorn, perforating the opening ceremony before a large attendance. The Vicar, the Rev. H. B. Firth, occupied the chair. The schoolroom bad been tastefully decorated, while on the stalls a large variety of articles was offered for sale. The VICAR said that no one took a keener interest in Church matters in the diocese of Chester than Mr. Littler. He was a man who placed the Church before all things and there were few men who had held as many of the church offices as Mr. Littler. That sale was being held for the purpose of raising funds for the objects of the church, L 56 being required for the Clergy Fund, £25 for the Churchwardens' Fund, while it was also hoped to have a substantial amount to band over to the building fond for the new St. Hilda's Church. Mr. LITTLER congratulated the workers who had shown in that room the result of a great effort and at the same time he congratulated the vicar on having such a band of people around him to second him in the work he undertook. He (the speaker), had ventured to take an interest in that parish because of the long association that their vicar had with the parish of Runcorn. No churchman or churchwoman should merely take a parochial interest in Church matters, but they should remember that they were members of the great Church of England, and whatever interest lay in any particular part of the work of that Charch that interest belonged to them all, and not only to those who were particularly concerned in the parmh The work of that parish was exceedingly gratifying to anyone who followed the progress of the Church of England, in finding the earnestness displayed in main- taining the efficiency of its present work, and also in their efforts to further extend it. There was nothing more important than the objects for which that sale vas being held. They in the Church of England always nembered that their churches were not merely preach. ing institutions, but they were places where the means of grace were to be obtained, and where kindliness and helpfulness arose. The members of that parish were to be commended not only for their efforts to maintain the statl of clergy, but for their further efforts to bring the work of the church right into the midst of the people, and he hoped that their labours would be crowned with success. Au it was the tendency of modern days to lessen the influence of the church there was no need for its to be warned against it, and as far as in us lay, to influence public opinion against the advance of such a disposition. Let them always be alive to see that nothing happened either in civil or Parliamentary spheres that would in any way lessen the ir finance of the Church:on the people of this coentry. (Applause). Mr. J. F. FOSTER in proposing a vote of thanks to Mr. Littler thanked him for his generosity in coming to open the sale. Mr. B. HEATH seconded and said that he hoped when they "put the abutters up "the stalls would have been c!eared.

The Method of Teaching the Bible. Not Merely to Read but to Picture.

As interesting lecture was deliverei by Dr. Thistleton Mark, of Victoria University, under the auspices of the Wariington Sunday School Uoion, in the Bold-street Wesleyan Schoolroom, on Thursday evening, when be took as his subject " The Bible ass vile to m.-thods of teaching." lie said that the . .

flible was a book based upon, and was the outcome of human experience. It appe..led to the test of experience. Although it was not a book to be learned off by heart it was a book to be tested and its results would act upon those tests. What we thought of the Bible depended to a great extent upon bow we were going to teach it. Our attitude to it was the attitude of men and women who had tried it. It was received in that it belonged to everyday life and it was the best business in life to read it and gather from it the message that it contained. If we intended to teach well from the Bible we must get its spirit and method. We should cultivate the habit of picturing the scene' that were there written down. They could hardly take a passage which was not suggestive of some panorama portraying human life. We could have a better power to prevent the story and to teaching the lesson if we net ourielves to picture it. The words should not be merely learned but we should view the scene and work from it. The Bible was practicable by appealing to the will of man and giving him fresh purposes. It gave us the power of choice with the result that the choice was emphasised there. They wanted the scholar to tabs an interest in its teaching by questioning and by con- tributing his part. GOLBORNE-STREET BAPTISTS.

Recognition of the Rev. H. Ogdeu . The Church and the World.

The Rev. 11. Ogden, who has entered upon his ministry at Golborne-street Baptist Church, was formally" recognised" and welcomed by the congre- gation at special services held on Thursday. In the afternoon, a sermon was preached by Or. Jr. Marshall, Priocipal of the Baptist College, Manthester,who took for his text the words " Receive him therefore, in the Name of the Lord." He sp.tke of the new minister coming to the church as a brother, a companion in labour,and a teacher, and urged the church officers and members to encourage him by regular attendance and prayer. . .

There was a large attendance at a meeting held in the evening under the chairmanship of Mr. B. Few. The speakers included the Revs. Hector V Thomas (secretary of the Lancashire and Cheshire Baptist Association), George Curtiss (Hill Cliffe), and J Chedburn (Latchford), Mr I B Garaide (altuachester), and Mr T M Morris (Oldham). The CHURCH SECRETARY (Mr. I'. Longshaw )outlined the steps which led to Mr. Ogden receiving the " call " to the pastorate, pointing out that he created a great impression when be preached at the Bible Class anniversary three years ago and that he had also given willing assistance to the church while it was without a pastor. If he received the loyal support of the con- gregatioo, they would BOOR be able to build up a church that would be a credit not only to the town but to the denomination to which they were proud to belong. They would shortly commence to pull down the old school and replace it with one of modern type and larger size. The church had also to be altered and altogether Mr. Ogden had a strenuous time before him. They hoped his ministry would see the dawn of • grand era in the history of that church and of all the Free Churches in Warrington and district. (Applause.) Mr. I. H. CIA RSIDS, of Manchester, then spoke of his intimate relations with the new pastor, extending over a number of years. They had fought side by side for great principles—principles which were perhaps unpopular tut which were of God. All principles, in the eyes of those who had not spiritual perception, were unpopular. He had seen Mr. Ogden laugh in the face of the enemy. They had shaken hands together in the front of a howling mob, put their hands in their pockets, squared their shoulder, thrown back their heads and grinned when all the forces of reaction and the devil had been against them. In Mr. Ogden that church would have a pastor who could not be fooled. If they were only prepared to play with holy things, there was no place for him. If they only wished to go half way, to adopt a dilletante attitude, they had no uEe for him. He (the speaker) was tired of hearing churches soy their pastor was dry and cold. Ile never knew a dead pastor who had not a aead congregation, and nine men out of ten had been killed by the pew, strangled by the indifference, apathy, stupidity and cowardice of tbeir congregations. They could not expect a man to run as a messenger of God if he Lad to carry a crowd upon iiis shoulders who did not want to run. The great mass of men and women to-day were sick, tired andldisgusted with the Christianity of the average church and man. The world was looking not for • theological Christ, but for a living, vital embodiment of the personality of Jesus Christ. " Don't tell me" continued the speaker " that every drunkard is a bad math I don't believe it, nor do I believe foes moment that every woman of the town is necessarily a bad woman.lou cannot dreg men and women into the Church tut you can love them into it. There is not a bad man on earth who is any worse than you probably would be if you had to be tempted as he is." Mr W. Tynan then song "If with all your hearts" very expressively,. The Rev. F. V. TFIOM as extended to the new pastor the greetings of the Lancashire and Cheshire Baptist Association of 30,000 member?, and also congratulated the congregation that they bad milled to their ministry their well-Leloved brother, Mr. Ogden. At Oldham Mr. Ogden bad been faithful, persistent and true In his wirk. He was not desirous of tickling the fancies of his congregation but of winning men and women to Christ. There was a great deal of unrest in the country at the pretreat time. Workingmen might have done with the Church, but the Church had not done with the workingmen. The rich and higher classes might have done with the Christian Church and ministry, but the Church was only beginning to deal with them. She had neglected them too long. (Hear, hear.) It wan the business of the Church to teach, preach and inspire, so that the characters of all classes in the industrial world should be like the character of Jesus Christ. Then there would bi no injustice and warfare. (Applause.) Mr. T. M. MORRIS, of Oldhara,spoke of Mr. Oeden's successful eight years' ministry in that town. When a church had got its central pillar right, it might with safety go anywhere. Their central theme moat be the Cross of Christ and the redemption of man was the end of their work and prayers. When they had finished with that, they could start with the platform with safety. It was difficult at times to distinguish between the Church and the world, between the men who professed and who did not profesa redemption in Jesus Christ. ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

The Ellery Band.

On Sunday evening the celebrated company of Americanised Italians known as the Ellery Band gave a special concert in the Royal Court Theatre before an audience which, though fairly numerous, would undoubtedly have been larger but for the very inclement weather prei ailing at the time it assembled. The visits of Sousa's famous band have made us familiar with the combination of brass and wood wind instruments, plus some very modern reeds of the saxophone order, which constitute what is called the military band of the present day, and, although comparisons are to be avoided as a rule, it is useful to note that in Mr. Ellery's assemblage playartrone realises all the musical pcssibilities of the more widely known band without experiencing those distractions of manner in the conductor and that thrusting of groups of instruments upon the attention that often 90 seriously disturb enjoyment of the pieces discoursed. In the Ellery Band one finds a maximum of sweetness and variety of tone colour, while precision and point in both attack and release are, as often happens in these combtnations, some- times carried so much beyond all bounds as to become positively painful. The latter objection is, however, one which is happily inherent chirfly in the ensembles, and while undue crispness cannot spoil much of the music of the "William Tell" overture for instance, it m.y, and did on Sundl evening, assert itself too obtrusively in the transcription of Liszt's second Hungarian Rhapsody of which an otherwise magnificent inter- pretation was given. Too much point, to, little soulfulness, and a rather rapid tempo also detracted somewhat from the band's playing of the Andante fr nn Beethoven's fifth symphony,—another number in which the military 'band combination of instruments realised in quite a wonderful degree much of the effect , which only the strings can give in full. But in the "1812 overture" the band really "found itself" as ' the French say, and Tschaikowsky's score received an interpretation that was at orce vivid, majestic, and musicianly, the magnificent climax of the work being finely realised despite, rather than thanks, to the distractions of red fire and the mimic discharge of fire-arms. There were two notable instrumental sob s—the aria " Softly awakes my heart" (Saint ' Saens) given splendidly on the trumpet ly Mr. D'Amico, and "0 star of eve" (Wagner) well played by a euphonium soloist. A tenor and a baritone vocalist,—Mr. Tom Wallace and Mr. Bayne Young respectively,—gave farther variety to the programme with some songs which though of no great intrinsic interest were both well sung and well received. The conducting of the Ellery Band by Signor di Girolamo 9988 beyond reproach in its complete control of the ' instrumental forces and the bringing out of latent , possibilities in artistic taste.

===== 0002601_19111104_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
" The Whip"

A Drury-lane drama at Warrington is somewhat of a novelty, and the enterprising management of the Royal Court Theatre, Warrington, are to be congratu- lated on having this week secured Mr. George Dance's company in that remarkable record-breaking produc- tion " The Whip," with its huge company, wonderful , scenery and effects, and specially trained horses and hounds which give the necessary realism to this rare sporting drama. There are no less than nine scenes in the three acts, and they are all presented with great lavishness, while such unique effects as a rail- way smash, with clouds if steam, and wreckage galore; the finish of a great race with the horses thundering neck and neck across the stage, are presented in a style which makes one wonder if there is any limit at all to the possibilities of stage production. The plot of" The Whip" is an exceptionally fine one, dealing with schemes and counter-schemes respecting a racehorse of that name. Captain Sartory., an unprincipled indivicht 11, is face to face with financial and social ruin unless the Ir,rse is defeated in the 2,000 guineas. Be therefore plans a risky scheme, and personally uncouples the horse box in which "The Whip" is travelling in order that it may be smashed up by an express train. The plot is foiled by Mrs. Beamish, who is in love with the trainer of the horse. Captain Sartorys then tiles to secure the arrest of the jockey on the racecourse, but this is foiled by the sympathetic crowd (headed by Lord Braneaster and the owner of the horsa), who make a rush for the detectives and no enable the kckey to mount the fatnons Whip and win the race in thrilling style. Some idea of the realism attained i n this drams may be gathered from the following description which appeared in one of the London papers when the play was revived in March, last year If you with to catch &glimpse of the great annual horse show at Olympia, if you would like a stroll through the Chamber of Horrors at Madame Tussand's, if you would see an express steaming at full speed across country, now thundering past wood and meadow, now disappearing into the dark recesses of a gloomy tunnel, if you would take your place or. the grand stand at Newmarket amid a crowd of notabili- ties and distinguished people, you can accomplish all this by the simple means of booking treats for " The Whip." We have nothingibut praise for the excellent work done by Mr. Dance's talented company, and the large audiences each night in Warrington testified in unmistakable fashion their appreciation of the leading

members in the many fine roles, which The Whip " provides. Miss Elsie Wood is bright and captivating as Lady Diana S.rtorys. the grandchild of Lord Beverley, owner of " The Whip," while Mr. Wernham liyott acts with force and finish in the companion role of Earl of Bmncaster, the victim of a matrimonial conspiracy, but finally the captor of the fair Diana's heart and. hand. Mr. Edwin Palmer is typical as the fine old hunting squire and artistocrat, Lord Beverley, and never lacks dignity in the most trying situations. ' Captain Sartorys, the villain of the piece, is a real sporting " wrong on" who alit inks no more from compromising a young village girl than he does of" backing a loser," and when driven to it caps his villany with the attempt against "The Whip," which results in ths train-wreck. Mr. Frank Powell pourtraye the role most convincingly, and raises many a laugh by his callous indifference to the consequences of his plotting and peccadilloes. Miss Lily Barclay aides him in his wicked schemes in true adventuress fashion, and a better Mrs. D'Aquila could hardly be imagined. The play is not devoid of humour, and the many comical passages between Mrs. Beam (Miss Naomi Neilson) and Tom Lambert (Mr. E. W. Avery) are a source of never ending amusement. Mr. Avely is • comedisn of rare talent and his share in the screamingly funny scene at Madame Tussand's is • great personal triumph. Another character which as particularly well interpreted is that of Joe Kelly, the unscupnlous, droll and broad-tongued Yorkshire book- maker. Mr. George P. Polscn makes a great hit in the part, and the same may be said of Mr. William Kershaw, as the back-sliding parem, Rev. Vernon Haslem.

MIDNIGHT BEDROOM STRUGGLE.

Lady s Terrible Experience at Warrington.

In the early hours of Sunday morning a sensational affair took place in the Roebuck Hotel when Mrs. Mary Ann Chrimes, an elderly widow and the licensee of the above hotel, on her departure to bed at 12.10 am, discovered a man biding underneath her bed. It appcare that the other occupants of the house bad retired, and she had already extinguished her light, but before entering her bed she looked underneath and thought that she saw a dark form, and as she placed her hand upon it she felt that it was warm. She made an effort to obtain some matches from the dressing table, when she was confronted by a man, who imnadiately attacked her. Gripping her by the throat her assailant threw her on to the bed and when she endeavoured to scream the man placed a pillow over her faee He then released Mrs. Chrimes, who called out for help to a maid in another room. Her cries drew upon her a second attack, and in the struggle a small table was knocked over. The com- motion aroused the household, and perceiving that someone was approaching the man bolted from the room. Mrs. Chrimes rushed to the window and her cries attracted Constables Tolley and Hawkins to the scene. The Officers were admitted to the hotel, lint no traces of the landlady's assailant could be found. Mrs. Chrimes stated that she knew the man by his basing on several occasions cleaned the windows of the h^tel. He bad no right on the premises and mast have secreted himself during business hours. He had jumped from the lavatory window on the se o3d floor and his right arm was fractured, one of his hips was seriously injured, and he was further suffering from cuts to the free. A MOTORLESS AEROPLANE.

Mr. Orville Wright, the famous American airman, has been experimenting with an aeroplane fitted with wings only, and without a motor. With a short run and the manipu- lation of levers, altitu.es of between 150 ft. and 200 ft. have been reached, and Mr. Wright has been able to remain stationary at this height in a fifty-mile gale. The longest flight yet reported has been one of ten minutes. Our picture shows Mr. Wright seated in his new biplane.

TORN BY HACKING COUGH.

Thought She was Dying until Rescued b:

Peps.

"I was BO bad with bronchitis that everybody thought I waa dying." These are the words of Mrs. Laura E. Clarke, of :7, Miltbrook Place, Widcamte, Bath, who has been rescued from an existence that was practically a " living death " by the wonderful breatheable Peps. "Two winters ago," Mrs. Clarke told a reporter, " I was laid up with a severe attack of bronchitis. A backing cough nearly tore me to pieces, and brought up phlegm which stuck in my throat until I was afraid I should choke. All day and night I was tortured. My chest was fearfully raw and sore, which in not surprising considering that I often coughed and struggled for breath from four until ten o'clock in the morning. After these bouts of coughing I woul/41 lay like • log, too exhausted to move, and with a violent, thumping headache. . . . .

" I persevered carefully with the doctors' medicines, but the cough wasn't lessened a Lit, sod I got so bad that everybody thought I was dying. My family were always on the look-out for something to relieve me, arid my daughter said she would buy me a box of I'eps if I'd take them. I promised, though I admit I didn't expect any relief this side of the grave. That first box a Peps was a great blessing ! Before I had finished half the tablets my throat and chest were so much easier that I slept all through the night. The cough, too, got less troublesome. I very naturally kept regularly to these wonderful Peps tablets, and every time I breathed their pine future down my throat I got relief. Besides getting rid of the awful cough I was at last freed from the choking phlegm, and lost the feeling of soreness from my throat and chest. The difference Peps made in me was really wonderful, and astonished us all.

WARRINGTON CHAPTERS. ),,,,,,,,,...„

THE PLAGUE IN CHESHIRE.

A MINGLED RECORD.

Warrington possesses no record of plague visitations, but entries made elsewhere give certain evidence that the town did not escape. Thus in the Manchester Constables Accounts of 1630 the item occurs: " Paid Is Sd. for the hire of a horse for S eeven Gee to goe to Warrington to bringe word whether itt were infected with the Plague." And in the Annals of Liverpool for 1645 it is ordered that a strict watch be kept on account of the sickness at Warrington. That the town suffered at that period is evidenced in rather striking fashion in the journals of the House of Commons, which contain an order that collections be made in all the churches and chapels within the city of London, and that two parts of the money be sent to the Mayor of Chester and the other part to Mr. Robert Massey of Warrington, to be distributed among the sufferers. This is under date June 29, 1647, and it is expressly stated that the gift is made because the city of Chester and the town of Warrington were grievously visited with "a plague of pestilence, and many were likely to perish of want if not relieved in an extra- ordinary way." The attention of Parliament betokens a serious state of affairs, and it is remarkable

The only passage in a register more poignant than this is cue written by the rector of Great Hampden who had the terrible task of recording the burials of his household. In those stricken days the church yards were closed and bodies laid in any field or garden. In the entry we can feel the added grief that followed upon burials in unmarked or scarcely marked graves, and the very natural anxiety that posterity should know and refrain from molesting the resting place of those who had been dear to him. We quote only part of the record. 1647 N.B. My daughter Sarah Lenthall was buried ye eleventh day of August Au : supra, I she came fro' London to Wickham and on ye Saturday only to are as and go t 3 return ye morrow and in ye afternoon to Wyckham againe, but then fell sick and en WEdnesday morning following being ye 11th of August about an hours before sun rise dyed of ye sickness, end no in ye evening we buried her in ye meade called ye kitchen meade by ye I edges de as you go done into it on yor lett hand, a little below ye pond at ye enterance into ye meade ; she was aged 14 years, eleven months and seventeen dayes—had she lived to Bartho'omew Day she hod Lin full 15 years of age. Suariotta Lactation my trite &pled this life Thursday evening about 8 a o'clock ye 26 of Avg-net, she died of ye sick, ens comfortably and in peace and was buried Auguot ye 27 by ber daughter Sarah. Ad.ian Leothall my souse a hovtfull yontman and were one and twenty years of age dept this life of ye sickness, Thursday morning a little ls fine day breake and was hurt. d at ye head of his sister Sarah's grave the Barre thy, being ye 2d of Septetnb.

Robert Leotball, Rector. A PRESCRIPTION AGAINST THE PLAGUE. MACCLESFIELD AND THE PLAGUE. Second only to Chester in their fight with the Plague were the townships of Maccles- field and Congleton. A municipal manuscript of Macclesfield dating from 1603 records some of the precautions taken. . _

THE PLAGUE-HERO OF MALPAS. The year 1625 witnessed a virulent out- break at Malpas. It is supposed that the infection was brought from London by Ralph Dawson, and within the month that followed his entire family was swept away. We referred in an article upon Malpas to the Richard Dawson who dug his own grave and died therein. This was one incident in the events of a terrible month, and the entry as it refers to the Dawson family may be copied here entire.

" Ward and watch was kept at every common passage out of the towne and at cross lanes neare adj?yning to ye towne to keep in ye townsmen.

"A -mariet every Thursdey was kept in an open place three quarters of a mile from ye town where two or three of the justices were weekly present, to see ye towne and country kept asunder and to see ye money, come, and other provisions distributed, 'which came from ye country either by tax or guift." The Justices had weekly notice which streets were infected, how many houses in every street, how many persons in each house and ye ability (for wealth) of ye parties."

Thomas Jefferie, servant to Thomas Dawson, of Bradley, butyed the 10th daye of August, in i the night, he died of the Plague. Richard, the sonnne of Thomas Dawson, of Bradley (that dyed of the Plague), buryed the 13th daye of August, in the night, 1625 nihil. Raffe Dawson sonne of the aforesayed Thomas, came from London about 25th July last past, and being sick of the plague dyed at his father's house, and so infected the sayd house and was buryed, as is reported, nears unto his father's house. Thomae Dawson, of Bradley, died of the Plague and was burped th' 16th days of August at 3 of the clocke, after midnight, Elizabeth, the daughter of the aforesayed Thomas Dawaon,died of the Plague of Pestilence, and was burped the 20th daye of August. Anne, the wyffe of John Dawson, sonne of the aforesayed Thomas Dawson, died of the Plague of Pestilence, and wan buryed the 20th of August. Diehard Dawson (brother to the above named Thomas Dawson of Bradley) being sick of the Plague and pereeyving he must die at yt tyme, arose out of his bed, and made his grave, and causing a his name, John Dawson, to cast some straweinto the grave, which was not farm from the house, and went and layed him down in the sayd grave, and caused clothes to be layd uppon, and aoe dep'tcd out of this world • this he did because he was a strong man and heavier than his slid nefew and another wench were able to burye ; he died about the 23rd of August 1625. This much he did I was credibly tould. John Dawson, aanaa a the above named Thomas, came unto his father, when his father sent for him, being eicke, and having layd him down in a dich, died in it the 20th daps of August 1625 in the night. Rose Smyth, servant of the above named Thomas Dawson, ard last of yt household, died of Plague, and was buryed by John Cook the sth daye of September near ye sayd house. It is not usual to insert in church registers anything save formal and necessary entries, but the clerk evidently felt that some narration should be made of the awful circumstances which swept away an entire family. Pathetic indeed lathe picture of the family burying each other one by one,the son in a ditch, the brotherin a grave of his own making, and an outsider coming in to close the ground over the servant who was last to be stricken.

The justices had every markett a bill (i.e. list) of ye names of such as dyed the weeke before."

FIELD GRAVES.

Few of the burial places of plague victims are marked by gravestones. Many are known and reverenced, but few inscribed stones remain. An interesting exception is to be found upon the moorland above Lyme Park and distant about a mile from it, where three gravestones mark the resting places of as many members of the Blakewell family. The are inscribed : John Rampson and his wife and three children left this life 1616. Think it not strange our bones ly here, Thine may ly thou knowest not where. E.:tutu-re 11AMPSON. Other stones are inscribed: Beare lyeth the body of Raphe Blakewall, who died july the 26 Ano dim 1646. Beare lyete the b_cly of Ellen Blakevrall who dyed july the 26. AOO dim 1646. Bete lyeth the body of Robert Blakevrall who died july 1. . Ano dim 1641'r. The history of the Plagues in England has never been written. Not even of the Great Plague of London have we a history for ' Dafoes "Journal of the Plague Year" is an imaginative sketch with no firm basis of fact and apart from the diaries of Pepys and Evelyn there is no contemporary record. It would seem that the scribes shrunk in sheer horror from the task.

Of the greater interest therefore are the tiny and scattered memorials which we have thought it worth while to gather here..lA.M.

FROM "1 LABOUR'S STANDPOINT.

By Philip Snowden, M.P. THE "SPLIT" IN THE LABOUR PARTY.

One day last week a Londois morning journal came out with a sensational state- ment about a " split " in the Labour Party and it followed up this announcement with the information that four members of the 'party had seceded and were about to form a new group. This journal is evidently determined to outdo the most sensational of the yellow press, for the following day it spread itself out to the extent of two columns with a detailed statement of the proposals of the coming Home Rule Bill—a statement which was immediately repudiated by responsible persons.

===== 0002601_19111104_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
THE OBVIOUS REASON.

The reason for the publication of the unfounded statements about the "split" in the Labour Party was quite obvious to those with inside knowledge. The report was intended to prejudice the chances of the Labour candidate in the Keighley division where the polling was to take place the following day. Although the inaccuracies and unfounded statements in the first report in the journal in question were contradicted by the persons concerned, the following morning—the poll- ing day in Keighley—the statements were put forward again with the audacious inven- tion that enquiries among Labour members had confirmed their accuracy. That a reput- able journal should be guilty of such conduct passes all understanding. I was quoted at having said to a representative of the journal that there were no dissensions in the Labour Party. There was not a word of truth in my having made any such statement to the journal.

The report about this " split " in the Labour Party said that 'certain members of the party had been called to account for their opposition to the Insurance Bill; that they had been ordered to vote for it or to

leave the party; that they had refused to withdraw their opposition; that they had decided to form a new party. One can quite understand a report being inaccurate in detail, though it is based on fact. But there is only one possible explanation of such an issue of complete fabrications as those just quoted. That explanation is that some evil disposed person sat down and deliberately invented . _

these statements. No member of the Labour

Party has been called to account for his opposition to the Insurance Bill; no member of the party has been either asked or ordered to vote with his party on the Insurance Bill; not a word has been said to me, neither have I said a word to anyone else about forming a . .

new party. The only business discussed-at the party meeting at which it is alleged all

this:tooli place Was the action of the-party.

on the GOvernment motion to appropritite all the time of the House of Commons, and also on the guillotine resolution. The division records of the House of Commons show that there were differences of opinion among the party on the question of supporting these motions, and out of this fact all the sensa- tional reports about "split'' in the Labour Party have been invented, not merely to provide the readers of the journal with a little excitement, but to serve the practical purpose of helping to defeat a Labour candidate. LEGITIMATE DIFFERENCES.

There are no divisions in the Labour Party beyond what all the world knew before this concoction was invented. The division records of the House of Commons are public property, and they show that the Labour Party never has been a united party in the sense that its members have always voted solidly. I have not analysed the votes, but I should say that in one-fourth of the divisions since 1906, members of the Labour Party have been in both lobbies. On some very important questions the Labour Party has been divided. It was so on the Education Bill, though there were very definite instructions from the Labour and Trades Union Congresses to support the national solution of this question. It has been so on the Navy Vote, where Labour members have voted against the majority of the party to suit certain of their constituents. Innumerable other instances might be cited. If there be differences in the Labour Party

on the Insurance Bill is no new thing. The position of those who may differ from the majority who have " adopted " the Insurance Bill is far stronger than that of

the minority who went against the party on the Education Bill, because in this case of the Insurance Bill, there is absolutely no doubt about it that the minority of the Labour Party who will not "adopt" the Insurance Bill are supported by the opinions of the overwhelming majority of the Labour Party in the country.

===== 0002601_19111104_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
FREEDOM NECESSARY.

No party could ever hold together for a month which insisted upon all its members voting as they were told by the official clique of the party. Every party must allow a certain latitude for the expression of individual opinion. Every party does it,and it is all the better for it. The complaint of the Labour party against the other parties has been that they forced their members into the lobby against their convictions. It will be a bad day for any party when that can be truly said to be its deliberate and regular policy. Men do not leave a party because of differences of opinion on one particular question. Only differences on the question of 'general policy could justify separation from one's party. The differences in the Labour party on the Insurance Bill are not fundamental; but nobody can help seeing that there are tendencies at work in the party which if allowed to go on will carry the party clear away from the course which it was formed to follow. If the Labour party has "adopted" the Insurance Bill because it believes it to be a measure worth supporting with one or two amendments, then differ- ences on that matter are not fundamental. But if the Insurance Bill has been "adopted;' not on its merits hut because the majority of the Labour party are under some obligation to help to keep this Government in office, then a very fundamental question is raised and one upon which it is impossible to com- promise. No difference of opinion on the merits of a measure can ever cause a " split" in the Labour party; differences of opinion on matters af policy can, and certainly would do so if there were any determined departure from the fundamental principle of the party, namely its absolute independence of other parties. WHAT INDEPENDENCE IS.

There is still, I find, a good deal of mis- conception as to what is consistent with independence in a Labour Party. A few days ago I discovered in a public meeting that there was a general impression among the Socialists present that it was considered a

breach of independence for the Labour Party in Parliament to vote with the Liberals under any circumstances. No measure which a Liberal Government could propose could possibly in any of its details be worthy of the support of the Labour Party. That was their idea; and therefore on all occasions the Labour Party in Parliament must vote against the Liberals, just to show that it has not and cannot have any dealings with them. That is very silly, and shows an incapacity for clear thinking which is deplorable. Political independence does not mean wrapping the cloak of self-righteousness around one and refusing to associate with any others. It means being free and independent to associate for a particular purpose with anybody who may be useful in gaining that purpose. It means being free and independent to use any Party to serve one's own purpose. An independent Labour Party which is relatively small in numbers must use other parties if it means to get anything done. The one thing to always keep in mind is to see that in whatever it does, the party does not in any way com- promise its absolute freedom of action. A Labour Party in Parliament is perfectly right in voting with a Laueral Government when it is gaining something by so doing, but a Labour Party is absolutely wrong and it is running straight on to the rocks when it supports a Government for the convenience of that Government and to its own detriment. That is the possible policy of a Labour Party which would certainly "split" it from top to bottom. THE RECENT BYE-ELECTION. The result of the bye-election in the Keighley division, following on the Kilmarnock poll, is highly satisfactory. It shows that the Labour movement is not going back. A more militant policy on the part of the Labour Party in Parliament would be of great assistance to the move- ment in the country. The workers are ready to respond to a strong and intelligent lead. But I confess frankly that the most surpris- ing thing about the Keighley result is that the Labour candidate should have received such a large poll. When the only difference which the ordinary elector can see between the Liberal leaders and the Labour leaders is that the former want the Insurance Bill with sixty-five amendments, and the latter have " adopted " the Bill as it is and only want one or two amendments at the most, I frankly confess that I see no reason why the electors should prefer a Labour to a Liberal candidate. If the Liberal Party is doing the work of the Labour Party then there is no need for the Labour Party. But if the Labour Party has no greater purpose than present day Liberalism it has indeed lost its vision, and it will experience the fate of all who have no ideal.

"We must try to find a little more typing work, and make it up that way." "I loathe typing. You know that, Helen. wish I was dead. No one cares."

"I care, Mary; and if we put our 'Moulders to the wheel it will all come right presently, and we bhall laugh together ores these hard times."

''l shall never laugh until I get some money, and that will be never. If my father hadn't been no stupid he'd have settled enough on me somehow to give mc a decent living: and if I hadn't been such a coward I should have done what he did and got out of it all long ago. I'm no use to anyone." ''Well, we won't say any more about it to- night," said Helen, rising to go to bed. "To- morrow morning you'll be able to take a brighter Tim of things." "I wish with all my heart that there wasn't going to be any to-morrow morning for nie at ail," was the gloomy response. "If you're wise you'll take warning by my case. You can see what the end will be if you go on waiting for something to happen that never will happen." Mary Peseott remained in this condition of doleful despair for a week or two, and then Helen noticed a change in her. She ceased to harp upon her own troubles, but found every posaible chance of urging Helen to break with Bulmer, prophesying dire dis- aster unless she did so.

And in the meantime, to Helen's conster- nation, her own work began to be uncer- tain and difficult to obtain. Three engage- ments a; accompanist at concerts at private houses were cancelled without any reason being given, and two firms who had em- ployed her constantly for typing took the work away. Bulmer came up to London again just after this had occurred, and when she told him he only laughed and said it did not matter now. Ile had an opening at last, he declared, and, if all went well, there would soon be no need for either typing or piano playing. "It came through my father's bankers," he explained. "I rich American, named Burrell, has a huge place about a hundred miles from Chicago, and he doesn't like the way things are going in the development of it, and wants to send some one out to give him s straight report." "You will have to go away, then?" cried Helen, her face falling at the prospect. "Only for a month or two at most. And when I get, back, there will be something here for me. He is buying big estates in the north, and I shall have the agency and the management. It's quite a thousand a year. Think of it, Helen, a thousand a year and just the work of all others that will emit me. Of course I shall have to do the Ameri- can business all right; but I'm not afraid about that. I'm to have £250 and all my expenses for that." "It is splendid," she agreed; hut there was no enthusiasm in her voice.

He was too tented by his good luck to notice this, and rattled on, making plan after plan for the future until he had in some de- gree infected her with his own feeling of de- light. "And now I'm going to take your breath away," he said at length, with a hearty laugh. "How?" "I want you to go with "Hulmer! she exclaimed, with a catch of the breath.

I said I should," he laughed, "and I have. But I am serious. It will be the best possible thing for us both. Throw up that dingy old West Kensington and all the click- clacking of the typewriting and take the plunge at once. We can get married very quietly here in London; you can have that Pescott girl with you—whom I don't like, by the way—and I can get a triend as best man who'll hold his tongue—" But your promise to your father. But. mer?"

only promised that we would not be married until I had a position good enough to marry on; and here it is. And who knows, when we once get out to the States we might find something even better than coming back here at all. Its a land of infinite possibili- ties, and we're both able to face things." But Helen shook her head. "I have had to fare troubles, and know what they mean, Bulmer. You might not get the appoint- ment here on your return, and then I should be a clog and a hindrance to your advance- ment."

"You're not afraid to take the risk, surely?' I for myself; but you have never known what it is to be poor." "Haven't I, by Jove. Why, I'm as poor as a church mouse," he interrupted. "I mean that you have never had to earn a slice of bread before you could eat it— that sort of poverty. It is bad enough for one, but it would be infinitely worm if there were two of us. It is not of myself that I'm thinking; but I could not bear to feel that I had dragged you down." "Is that all the faith and trust you have in met" he asked with a laugh. "Don't say that; don't even hint at it, but I have been at close grips with poverty; am again now, it seems, and no one who has sot felt the pinch can understand what it really moons. Besides, there are other reasons," she added, after a pause. "'You— you don't know me yet." "I know that there is only one woman in the world for me," he cried, slipping his arm round her waist and drawing her to him. "My Helen," he whispered, as he kissed her. She put her arms on his shoulders and looked into his eyes. "I have never even told you the story of my life," she said _ _ .

"My 'dearest, I have you, and don't care two straws about your story. Do what I ask you, Helen! Let us he married at once, and come out with me to America. It is best for us both."

"How you tempt me," she whimpered, smiling, but she shook her head. "And how I should love to go with you "It doesn't look much like it," he replied ruefully. "I'm afraid you are on my father's side rather than mine.'

ty the morning Sir Gregory had changed his mind again. The confession which had appeared feasible in the darkness and aoli- tude of the night bore a very different look when the actual time arrived for it to be made.

He had at first thought of taking Helen to his study after breakfast anth telling the truth to her alone, and asking her forgive- ness; but he found himself unequal to the task. Then he resolved to tell his wife first, and leave her to tell Helen and the rest. He himself would go away for a time. That appeared the simplest way, but • difficulty arose in regard to tht. Lady Tre- vellock was not so well in the morning, and any disturbing news might have a very bad effect upon her. He welcomed the respite, and let the day pass without speaking. It was not his fault, he told himself. It would be hazardous to distress her in her weak state, and he must wait at least until • she was better. It would not be more than a day or two.,at most, and no harm could come by putting off his revelation for a few days. Meanwhile he found last his conditional consent to the marriage had been welcomed by Bulmer and Helen. Bulmer was glad of the chance of proving the sincerity and strength of his love, and liked the thought of having to work for her. Helen was quilt willing to wait for him, and altogether the arrangement was one which appealed to them both favourably. Sir Gregory then began to find some ease in the thought that he could make Helen's position and future his special care. He would watch over her and see that her life was made easy and smooth until the time came when the two could be married—if it over was to come.

He began to persuade himself also that his first duty was to his wife. If the truth were told, and he had to admit that be had no right to the Chase, and they were compelled to leave there, he had no means of support- ing her. She was a confirmed invalid, and had grown accustomed to the enjoyment of all the comforts which the Income from the estates yielded; they were necessary to her; and to cut her off from them might involve her death.

The doctors had warned him often that only the greatest care and freedom from trouble and worry would preserve her life, and he was bound to think of all that. Bub mer himself would have been the first to urge such a course. And then there was the consideration that as Helen herself knew nothing of her rights. she was not actually suffering by the loss of them. She %vas happy in her work and inde- pendence. She liked the life she lived; and by giving his consent to the marriage he had added the element of hope to her life, which made it sweeter than ever. Other considerations occurred to him readily when he began in this way to per- suade himself that, after all, silence was the best course. Helen herself would not like the thought of there being any disgrace attaching to the father of the man she was to marry. And thus he came gradually to the conclusion that, at any rate, nothing should be done in a hurry. He would wait and see v. hat occurred. It was always open to him to tell the truth and set matters right, and lie would say nothing until necessity com- pelled him, or a favourable opportunity came for speaking. Helen went hack to London, therefore, in Ignorance of the wrong done her, and act about her work with a heart lighter than ever as the result of her visit to Bulmer's home. Bulmer went up with her on the pre text that it was necessary for him to lose no time in finding some sort of employment. Two days later Mrs. Caulfield found occa- sion to speak to Sir Gregory on the change in the position of affairs. "I find myself in a very awkward position, Sir Gregory, and want your advice. Lady Trevellock is very anxious for me to stay on here for a time, but I scarcely know what to do."

"I hope you won't think of going, at least until Mary is better," he said anxiously. "She is so fond of you." "But things are all altered—you what I mean, Bulmer's engagement. For me to be here when he is at the Chase would— well, you can see," and she finished with a gesture of embarrassment. "My dear lady, you know my wishes. but—" and he sighed. "I have no wish, of course, except for his happiness. Do you think this girl will rerrlfs, make him happy?" . .

Sir Gregory threw up his hands with a gesture of doubt. "May I speak whel is in my mind?" she continued. 'lf I may, I don't think she will. I mean, of course' that he has been accus- tomed so long to luxury that any change will he serious." .

"He is very much in lore with her," said ".:r Gregory, wry tactlessly. Mrs. Caulfield winced inwardly at the thrust, but replied with a very frank smile. "If you think she will make him happy, I can perhaps help in getting him an excellent appointment. I have many friends who would gladly do what is vecessary. But of course it would not do for him to know that it is my doing." "I think you are one of the beat of women."

She laughed. Not quite that, perhapt ; but at any rate I will see what I can do, and it shan be done without my hand showing. But I am going to be very frank. You know how much I had set my heart on winning his love; and I am American enough not to give in without • struggle. I still have hopes that time may cure him of this infatuation—for I can call it nothing ekie, can you?—and if the two were tested by a separation for a while, don't you think it would be best in all interests? I do."

She was watching him very keenly, and perceived the effect of the suggestion. It was clear to her that he did not desire the mar- riage; but she wished to know how far he would go in the effort to prevent it. "I could not do anything against my. son, Mrs. Caulfield. He is of age and able to Judge for himself in inch a matter; and I could not Interfere, of course. But I say candidly at once that I would a thousand times rather have had you for my daughter-in-law than the woman he has chosen. But he has chosen." "Absence makes the heart grow fonder, re. inember."

CHAPTCR VL NARY PESCOTT.

Miring the fortnight which followed Helen's visit to the Chase she saw a great deal of Buttner. They returned to London together, and he remained there, "looking for something to do," an he put it; and meeting everywhere with failure. He Ind heaps of friends, but none of them would be- lieve that he was serious in his desire to find the means of earning his own living. His easy life at Eton and Oxford bad been the worst of all preparations for the strenuous work of any career; and the fort- night served to bring home to him in some degree the actual difficulties to be faced. He was beginning to realise the full effects of the change in his position. anti Helen was too shrewd not to see his disappointment and chagrin. "No one will take me seriously." he ex- claimed more than once. "They only laugh when I say I want work, and don't care what it is."

"It is sure to be a little difficult at first," she replied gently; and I expect you will have to settle in your own mind the kind of work you wish to get." This was common sense, of course; but he could not yet appreciate it. "That's just it. I will try anything,; but what I could really do I do not know.'

"I expect that the men you know are not business men," she suggested. "One or two of them are. Stock Exchange and manufacturers and that sort of thing. But they all want to know how much money I can invest; and when I tell them none at all, they laugh and advise me to go abroad and try my luck in the States or the Cob- me,, or some such rot."

The net result of the fortnight was there- fore that, although he interfered in some degree with Helen's work, he returned to the Chase discouraged and not a little dis- mayed by the difficulties. Helen had to net to work harder than ever to make up for the time which Bulmer had taken up, and she was not a little troubled on Ins account.

She had a friend, Mary Pewit, who shared her little flat in West Kensington, and the latter was quick to perceive Helen's uneasiness.

This girls experience had been something like Buttner's. Her father had been a well- to-do solicitor, and she had been reared in idlencos and comfort. He had, however, be- come intolved in heavy speculations, and had used his clients money, and rather than face the disgrace, had taken his life, leaving the daughter to do the best she could. She had become • governess, therzfore, and having neither the gift of teaching nor the requisite training. had earned • very pre- carious living. But she could Ilge a type- writer, and she helped Helen in this work. She was s•me years the elder of the two, and her experiences had left her discontented, crabbed, and bitterly envious of all who were better off than herself ;and her one con sumiug desire was to get money. By the visit of Consul this week, Warringtonians have an excellent opportunity of witnessing the intelligence and aptitude for bling taught of the chimpanzee. THE BOARD OF TRADE AND THE COST OF LIVING.

Some articles have gone up solely on account of the very dry year through which we have passed. For example', it was inevitable that dairy products should become dearer ; but the enhanced price of milk and butter and cLeese reed not necessarily be permanent. Bacon, another very common article of consumption, ban fluctuated very much during the last three or (cur yearn, and it is always possible that dearneu of this kind may—be speedily rectified. The bacon pig Uppers to bean extremely prolific animal and, if the prcuniary reward were sufficiently tempting, farmers would very quickly place large supplies on the market. It wan unavoidable, too, that meat should go op, simply because the pastures were scorched and the cattle leprirred of their natural food, while artificial feeding- stuffs became more expensive. Wheat, on the contrary, has not risen as much as was at one time expected, and those who have it to sell are by no moans sanguine that the markets mill be in their favour. Thus the facts are apparently conflicting. and it can at least do no harm to hold an intelligent enquiry" into the changes Ithat have occurred.—" Country Life.

There was a previous con- viction against the prisoner who said that he was sorry for what had occurred.—The Bench sentenced him to four months hard labour, the Chairman stating that it was most cowardly for a man to assault a woman in such a manner.
