_ COMPENSATION FOR A LYMM NURSE.

sou rH MANCHESTER [GUARDIANS TO P AY. In the Manchester County Court on Monday, Emma Mostoo, of Cherry-lane, Lyrnm, formerly a nurse in the Withiugton Hospital, claimed an award under the Workmen's Compensation Act from the South Man- chester Board of Guardians on the ground that she was totally incapacitated from work by an accident which occurred in the hospital on July Li of last year. Mr. T. B. Leigh, instructed by Mr. Arthur Browne,solicitor, Warrington, was counsel for the applicant, and Mr. Percy Jordan appeared as solicitor for the respondents. Dr. Judson Bury assisted Judge Mellor, K.C.,as medical assessor. Mr. Leigh said that on June 13th of last year, Miss Moston entered the Withington Hospital as a nurse. A month later, as she was handing some hot soup to an inmate, the latter spilled it over her right hand and arm, scalding them badly. When the case was pre- viously before the Court in September the Guardians raised the defence that the present condition of the arm was produced by his client's misconduct in strapping or tightly bandaging the arm. Judge Mellor I understand they do not suggest misconduct, but that whatever she did she did honestly with the intention of helping herself. Mr. Jordan: Yes. I allege no fraud. The applicant, in the course of her [evidence, said that the only previous accideet she had had to her right band sass a bite on the thumb Billeted byla lunatic in an asylum where she was nursing, about six years ago Blood poisoning set is, hot not sufficiently to take her off duty. Since the last accident she had never applied any bandages or straps to her arm, but afterlbeing dismissed by the Guardians on May 28th she went into the Liverpool Infirmary, where, among other treat- ments, the arm was tightly bandaged for a fortnight or three weeks. Dr. Richmood,a former president of the Pathological Society, said he bad studied and written on the subject of lymphatic diseases for many year.. This was undoubtedly a case of lymph tr lema caused by the scald the applicant bad received. The swelling might fluctuate from time to time, but the arm would never recover. No amount of strapping or tight bandaging was capable of producing tech a condition, because the effects of tight bandaging passed away within 45 hours. The Judge said that he could corroborate this because, ashen his own arm was in plaster of Paris for seven weeks, his band swelled considerably, but the swelling disappeared after the removal of the plaster,

11

===== 0002601_19111104_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
IL

Dr. George Wright said that he had been called hr to examine the arm of the applicant, and as he knew of no disease which could have led to such a condition be concludel that it was due ta some physical or mechanical obstruction. In hit opinion the arm was not permanently incapacitated, and by proper treat• merit it might be made quite serviceable. At present, he agreed, it was absolutely useless. Cross-examined, Dr. Wright admitted that tight strapping would have to be continued for a very long time before swelling would become permanent, and that if the condition of this arm were due to tifht strapping the process would have to he still gotog _

Dr. C. M. Craig, assistant medical officer at the Withington Wotthouse Hospital, said that svhen the applicant was under his care he came to the opinion that she was malingering. Dr. P. R. Wrigley, who also treated her at the Workhouse, said he did not wish to suggest that she had deliberately malingered, hut that sometimes this kind of thing was done in hysteria. The Judge said he could understand people saying they could not sleep at nights and wanting to stay in ' bed on wet. days. but it seemed inconceivable that ant- one should go on deliberately causing herself intense pain for a year and a half. The case was a difficult one cad serious, because the charge against the some was really one of fraud • if she bad been guilty of malingering, it must have been with tt.re intention of securing compensation from the Guardians. People who alleged fraud in a court of law must prove it completely. After reviewing the evidence on both aides, he decided that the charge of malingering bad not been proved, and that, as the applicant was totally incapacitated, she must be awarded compensation at the rate of 12s. a week as from May 28 last, when her employment with the Guardians ceased. Coats would be allowti on the higher (B) scale. Mr. Jordan mentioned that the Guardians had only come to court on the strength of the medical reports made to them. These had been sifted, and they were quite satisfied.

NEW SEWERAGE SCHEME.

LOCAL GOVERNMENT BOARD INQUIRY.

Mr. A. W. Brightmore, an Inspector of the Local Government Board, visited the Lymm Council Office. on Tuesday to inquire into the application of the Urban District Council to borrow £9,000 for the purposes of sewerage and sewage disposal and £250 for the provision of the stables and cartshed at the Council's yard at Whitbarrow-lane, Lymm. Mr. T. Ridgway appeared on behalf of the Council and there were also present Mr 0 G Smith (chairman of the Council), the Rev 0 II Thurston, Messrs Groves, Gotobed, Davies' Lockyer, W ?dullard (clerk), D Mort (surveyor), W 111 Beckett (engineer for the new scheme), Dr De Jong (medical officer), and Dr Young (county raedical officer). Mr. Nltillard said that the area of the parish we, 4,375 acres, the population at the last census being 4,909 and the rates equal to ss. in the £. Mr. Ridgway said that the whole of the proposed works were in the district. The buildings in connection with the stables, which would adjoin the Council offices, complied with the byelaw, but there were no buildings in connection with the sewerage scheme. At the present time the whole of the cartage of the Council was hired, at the rate of 7a. per day, for man, horse and cart, and it was thought that it would be greatly advantageous to the Council to have their owa horses and vehicles as they could be kept in constant use. It was proposed to erect a stable for two homes, and a cart shed, the existing shed being very low. The existing material would be need as far as possible in the new erection, the cost of which was estimated at £250. It was necessary that horses should be available in the early morning to deal with the nightsoil, the Council having received numerous complaints about this matter from people whose bosiness compelled them to be out early in the morning, while the horses could aln be used for the fire engine. Mr. Gotobed said that numerous complaints were received by the people who, having to catch trains to business early in the morning were confronted by this nighteoi I which was a great source of annoyance.. Mr. Ridgway said that the loan for the present sewerage scheme was obtained in 1882 when the sewage farm and the syphon were erected, that scheme having lasted until the iiresent except for a slight alteration in 181r3. The main difficulties had arisen through the great amount of sewage that had to be contended with, the outfall works tbus becoming blocked and the sewage being apt to escape. Mr. Mort said that he had had considerable trouble with the present system in different parts of the parish the at aver haying had to be cleaned ont practically from one end to the otter. At present it was incapable of dealing with the storm water, being likely to get flooded. Toe manholes were cleared out every fortnight but if there was a big storm it was impossible to get into them. The joints of the sewer were badly made, the sewer thus taking additions, water to what was sent down the mains. In the Heatley district a new salt works had been erected which was responsible for more sewage than had previously been dealt with.

===== 0002601_19111104_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
TM: NEW SCJIEML

Mr. Beckett said that it was proposed to abandon the upper portion of the existing 15. inch main outfall newer from Whibarrow.road to the bridge under Reddish lane and to carry out the following new works : (a) New outfall works near Sow brook to deal with the eastern portion of the district, which contains a population of about 3,000. (td The diversion of the lower portion of the exist- ing 12-inch newer from the village from the abandoned , 15-inch main sewer to the proposed new outran works.' (C) A new 18-inch and 15-inch main outfall sewer from the proposed out fall works to Rushgreen-road with a storm overflow to the existing 24-inch sewer. (1) A new 12-inch and 9-inch intercepting sewer from Rushgreen-road in a southerly direction across the fields and the Bridgewater Canal to Sutcb-lane (picking up the existing sewer from Higher-lane and Grammar School-road land thence ins easterly direction across the fields to Oughrington-lane and along Oughtrington-lane and Higher-lane to the Jolly Thresher Inn. (,) New 9-inch sewers in Higher-lone between Grammar gollool-road and Oughtrington-lane, and the taking up of a abort length of existing 9 inch sewer across the fields from the Mission Room, and re-laying

the same at a ereater depth in order to improve the. gradients in Higher-lane. (f) A new 12. inch sewer from the bottom of Sandy- lane at the junction with Rush Green-road and across the fields to Mill-lane and up Mill lane to Oak Villa

Farm. 10) A 12-inch cast-iron pipe sewer tunnelled under the Canal to take the piece of the existing 12-inch syphon on the 12 inch Branch from the village. (0) The enlargement of the existing ejector pump- ing station at Heatley and the erection of an additional ejector with a capacity of 100 gallons per minute. Of the present population of 1,000 about 2,000 would con- tinue to be dealt with at the existing outfall works and 3,003 at the proposed new works at Sow Brook. Although the population had been almost stationary for the last 20 years it appeared probable that there would be a considerable increase in the Heatley district; in the near future, and the new works had been designed to deal with 90,000 gallons per day —equal to 20 gallons per head for 4,500. The site to be acquired was nearly 12.1 acre', of which about 101 ac-es would be laid out as a filtration area and the remaining two acres to be occupied by tanks, roads' &a., The works were as designed that at any future time when the population exceeded 4,000, percolating filters could be constructed on the North side and thus treat efficiently any excess flow of sewage. Mr. 0. G. Smith, the Rev. G. It Thurston, and the Medical Officer gave evidence as to the necessity of the scheme. . . .

There was no opposition to the applications Mr. T. Ridgway proposed a vote of thanks to the Inspector which was seconded by Mr. Gotobed, the. Inspector briefly responding.

ST. HELENS.

ST. HELENS TECHNICAL SCHOOL STUDENTS' SUCCESSES.

A highly-satisfactory report has been submitted to the St. Helens Higher Education Committee on the results of the examinations at the Technical School. The school is carried on in the Gamble Institute, but, notwithstanding its size, the Board of Education have recommended that the institute should be enlarged. Out of 1,007 there were only 213 failures, against 201 out of 35$ in the previous year. This year 795 (mead (292 first-class), against 652 last year. The Board of Ethic stion have awarded a atudent the bronze medal for first-class honours in metallurgy; three bronza medals and three national book prizes for machine drawing, one national book prize and one commendation for art works, and have accepted ten drawings for art teachers' certificates. One student gained second plaza with a silver medal and the 503. prize for the City and Guilds of London Institute. In the Union of Lancashire •ni Cheshire Institutes' examinations thirteen first places were gained, as well as numerous sacoodi and thirds and a student qudified for a colliery manager's csrtifl- cate and carried off the Banbury gold medal for shorthand.

Address on Rs Work and Objects

A large attendance of the members of the Warrington Branch of the United Kingdom Commercial Traveller" Association assembled at the Lion Hotel on Saturday evening, when an address was delivered by Mr. Thomas Mountain, of Keighley (the chairman of the Associa- tion), upon the work and °News of Use Association. The speaker was supported by Messrs. H. Mercer (chair- man), F. Plumptoo chairman), W. P. Ashton (treasurer), W. G. Hayes (secretary), and W. le Walker (assistant secretary). The CHAIRNIAN read a letter of apology for non- attendance from Mr. W. Peter Bylaw's, the president of the Warrington branch. Mr. MOUNTAIN said they were all members of an honourable profession, and were all prepared to do what they could to further the interests of the members of that profession. We lived in a progressive age. Great Britain had been called a na' ion of shopkeeperr, but he thought we were also an industrial nation and a nation of producers, and the interests of the couotry could not be better ser,red than by those who weris interested in it doing all they possibly could to further the nation's+ commercial interest There was • great deal of talk about specialisation—specialising is pro- duction, in order to meet foreign competition. He maintained that if it was necessary to specialise in order to produce it was necassiry to specialise in order to distribute. It was no U 33 their being ii nation of producers if they were not able to specialise as pro- ducers and he thought that Britiel trade could not be better enhanced thus by their making the best and most successful commercial trevellea. He was proud to say that the commercial travellers in this country were an ever-increasing body, the total practically reaching Demo° as compared with 41,05.1 in Mt. If the producer was granted the right to combine and organise in order to benefit the workingclass why should not they be granted those COOC•311b0S ? He (the speaker) claimed that the cemmerc-al travellers bad every right to combine in order to further their interests. The result was that they were now progressing on every hand. They must not however, forget the pioneers who did the spade work, svhoalthongh they did not make the pro- gress that was anticipated, they could not do less than eulogise them upon the work they din accomplish. Lney had now taken the right step in the right direc- tion, i.e., in the interests of self-improvement. They bellow 1 that by so. embining they were furthering the Interests not only of the commercial traveller but of , the employer, each of whom they believed depended , upon the other for their prosperity and proven. The commercial trtvellers' combination, which meant strength, enabled then to accomplish what could riot be adopted by them as individuals. The Suffrage •Challenge.

Father Day and Socialism.

Sir,— It is always interest:l"F to read dircoorses upon social and industrial questions sr hen delivered by ministers of the luistian religion, so seldom do they do so. The one by Father Day is doubly interesting bee Auto of his outspokenness and to me as • Socialist, it is refreshing to rend criticism of Socialism and the Socialist movement from such a source. Commenting tiFon the recent help or revolts Father Day said " The strikes which were simul- taneous in Autrrria, Spain and other ecuntries could not be interpreted as expression f beat discontent with condition,. of industry. 1 here wets trt such thing. hut were in reality tie work r f the Socialists of the world who incited animosity and brought revolution."

To me, as a Socialist. this statement hardly comes within the full meaning of the term" fair." Concern- ing whether discontent with conditior a cf industry was the cause of the recent revolts I think the average man-in-the-street well qualifiid to judge. My own impression is that it was a righteous protest against Pegradiog conditions of life aid a striviog after a better standard cf living. But when we Socialists come across such statements as' it win in reality the work of the Socialists of the world who incited animosity and brought about revolution' some of us think a reje inder is necessary t explain our position In the first place the Socislists are not ashamed of the fact that they try to wake the people discontented, for so long as the people ale satisfied with slum dwellings adulterated food, starvati n wages, ugly towns,no social reformer can ever hope to make condi- tions betterlt is not the fact that Father Day accuses us of making discontent that I ci iticize this snit- meat, but it is the manner in which it is put. Father Day conveys the imprestion to me that he believes the Socialists are a class of people who are not satisfied unless stirring up trouble when there is no need for it. If that were so I should immediately disassiciate myself from such a party. So far as I can see the Socialist party as a political and economic party are a strictly scientific party, critici-it g the capitalist system and advocating Socialism as a remedy with its faults and excesses even as the CM istian church. We point out that the private ownership of capital must inevitably bring about the unequal distribution of wealth so much deplorid by Father Day. And we say that to remedy this evil the source of wealth production, the land, railways, mines. Ac, must be owned and con- trolled by the people for the people, and our speakers are prepared to meet even Father Day in debate upon this point. Now the Sociallats appeal to the general public in a perfectly legitimate way. By street corner meetings, by newspapers, by pamphlets, leaflets, demonstrations, Ac. And by this means we reach those members of society who are the chief sufferers with a fair measure of success. For in spite of the fact that we are not as strong numerically as the Liberal or Tory parties we have it on the authority of Lord Selborne that cur influence is greatrr than the Tory Party. Our propaganda has revolutionised politics, compelling Parliament and parsons alike to consider more social problems. That the workers are discontented and also that they do not as yet know how to properly direct their powerful forces admits of no doubt. But we Socialists do not believe in strikes only as an expedient. We say the people have a mightier weapon than the strike. It is the vote. We say until the people control their industry for themselves materiel condi,ions will not improve much in spite of Lloyd-Georges anti-socialist legislation. We ray Parliament can bring ab ut Socialism and we also say to long as the people elect the owners of land, railways, mines, Ac, the people cannot expect anything d.fferent than the private ownership of the means of life. The rcople have clear these men out of Parliament if they want to control industry for the people's benefit. To quote Father Day, "Society was essentially an ethical organ. All is parts are subject to the moral law, which acting through the State ought to govern and control the whole people." When the people realise this some- thing will be dcae.—Yours LABOR OMNIA VINCIT.

P.S.—What are the Clurches doing toward i bring- ing this function into idealistic and perfect operatiou? L

STRIKE RIOTING AT EARLESTOWN. Assizes Sequel to Attacks on Signal Boxes.

In the course of his charge to the Grend Jury at Liverrool Assixes on Wednesday, Mr. Justice Avory, after referring to cases of rioting in Liverpool, said that in another charge aoainst Al'Mion and others there was evidence that the r:ot was in connectioo with the strike of railwaymen which took place on the 19th August. One of the persons accuaed—David Holme—was a signalman in the employ of the London and North-Western Railway Coropsny, and svas • trtmtee of the local branch of the Amalgamated Society of Railway Servants. On the night of the 19th August a disorderly crowd was assembled on the embankment a the railway at Earlestown, and two Isignal boxes on the line were,for all practical purposes I demolished. All the glass in the windows an, smashed by the stone-throwing, and the signalmen who were on duty is the two boxes were compelled for fear either of their lives or injury to their persons, to leave their dotiev. The acmsed persons—krMinn, Appleton. and Merritt—were identified by the police as either taking port in the stone-throwing or encouraging others in doing RJR:lime was identified as heiog in the crowd and maizing one of an observation which would indicate that he was at least approving, if not encouragiog,what was being done. His own statement was that he was only looking on ; that he was not encouraging it ; and that, is fact, he had advised the railwaymen not to do anything of the kind. It was for the grand:jury to say whether Elulme, as one of the crowd who was apparently doing nothing to prevent what took place, could to placed on trial as one of the parties to what in law undoubtedly

CONSTITUTED A RIOT. " I hope," added the Judge," that no one can be found to suppose that the existing law of this oountrg is not sufficient to deal with eithar or both of those cases, and I hope that no One will ever doubt that It is the duty of those who are charged with the

maintainance of law and order in this country to avail themselves of any force that is necessary in order to put a stop to such scenes as occurred on these two nights of the 13th and and 19,11 of August, when property was not only seriously damaged, hut when on the lost occasion the lives of passengers on the railway might have been, ,if not actusily Eacrificed, at all events seriously endangered by the conduct of these persons whi drove the sigoalmen from their punts of duty." There los', coot:1.11d his lorddlip, • further charge against the pis, nem in the Earles:own cue cf intimidatmg the aid mimeo by preventiug their dela I; that which they had • legal right to do—to remain at their posts of duty. MR. CROSFIELD AMONG " MEN OF THE DAY

A "Mayfair ." Cartoon.

The popular London Society journal " Mayfair " has this week included Mr. A. H. Crosfield in the" Mayfair gallery of Men of the Day," and published a full-length coloured cartoon of the ex-Liberal Member for the Borough by " Pip." The drawing represents Mr. Crosfield in golfing attire, with a sandhill bunker as a background, and is altogether an excellent portrait. In the same issue "Junius Junior" writes of Mr. Crosfield as follows:

The popular belief is that pre-eminence in any pursuit is only to be attained by those who specialise in it; more particularly is this held to be true in the case of politics, sports and commerce; exceptional proficiency in any one of the three is commonly sup- posed to be incompa'ible with proficiency in the others. As always, however, an excep- tion can be found to prove the rule. A. H. Crosfield, the late Liberal Member for the ancient town of Warrington, who won the seat at two successive elections—a feat no Liberal had previously accomplished—has succeeded in everything to which he has turned his hand. A brilliant and accom- plished orator, he is, at the same time, not only an admirable man of business, but is; also, one of the leading figures in that cos- mopolitan circle of golfers which has done so much to bring into touch men of different nationalities.

In 1894, when he was barely 19 years old, and within two years of his leaving Upping- ham—he had previously been at Malvern— the chief authority and responsibility for the conduct of the great alkali and soap business his grandfather founded nearly a century ago devolved upon his shoulders. Within a few months one of these crises in commerce began, in which the weak must inevitably go to the wall and which only the very capable survive. A. H. Crosfield soon proved that I he belonged to the latter—not to the former category. So conspicuous, indeed, was his success that, before he was 23, he was invited by a unanimous vote of the representatives of the allied trade associations of the Northern and Midland Counties of England to be their honorary secre- tary, a position which enabled him to acquire a very wide and valuable experience of commercial diplomacy. In the twelve years succeeding his taking over charge of the family business he took the most active part in the work of building up what was, to all intents and purposes, a vast new commercial structure, on the modern lines, with ramifications all over the world, capable of indefinite expansion. Only then did the deep interest he had always taken in politics—an interest probably inherited from his father and grandfather and still remoter forbears closely identified with public affairs —begin to find full scope. In 1900, having been invited to stand for Warrington, he had his first political battle. The next General Election saw him at Westminster, and at the third attempt Warrington repeated her verdict in favour of her strong, convinced Liberal son. At the last Election there was a small majority against him. In the domain of sport A. H. Crosfield's successes have been equally remarkable. At billiards, fives, lawn tennis and golf, and other games of skill he is a skilled performer. When he was in practice some fifteen years ago he was considered one of the finest amateur billiard players in the North of England, and he proved his strength in a memorable contest at Monte Carlo in a match with Mr. Gaskell, a former winner of the Amateur Championship of England, and one of the best amateur planers of whom there is record. But, latterly, it is golf, of which he acquired the rudiments nearly thirty years ago from Mr. Charles Hutchings, he has principally devoted his sporting energies. At Pau, at Cannes, and in the Engadine— wherever, in fact, devotees of the Royal and ancient game most do congregate —the name of A. H. Crosfield is one to conjure with. He joined the Cannes Club within two or three years of its establish- ment by the Grand Duke Michael, and for the past six years, at the invitation of his ilmperial Highness, he has been its captain. I In Switzerland, too, he has taken a very active part in establishing the game, and for I eight years has been acting president of the I Swiss Golf Association, a body which has the active support of many who bear the most I distinguished names in the Almanac de Gotha. He it was, also, who, fifteen years since, inaugurated the first Continental Golf Championship. He was likewise instru- mental in starting the Swiss Golf Champion- ship—an annual competition which has excited the keenest rivalry, and is destined to be of ever-increasing interest. His own exploits on the green are innumerable, but that of which he is most proud is probably the Open French Championship, v•hich he won at Paris in 1905 with many formidable competitors in the field. Nor does his devotion to sport debar him from cultivating less strenuous pursuits. He has a keen appreciation of what is excellent in literature, music and art.

The Crosfields belong to good old North- Country stock, and can trace their unbroken descent from the early years of the sixteenth century. One of them, who died at Kendal in 1667, had joined the Quakers, and was succeeded for a century and a-half by a long line of Quakers. In the hands of A. H. Crosfield the family fortunes and honour are not likely to suffer any diminution." Lancashire and Cheshire lastltutes.

The most haeresting appeal brought before the committee was that of Messrs. Greensll, Whitley and Co., Ltd., sgainst the present assessment of Wilders- pool Brewery. Under a previous valuation the figures stood at £3,, siS gross ; .C.. 2,349 rateable, but on a re-valuation by Mr. Gunaon they were reinced to .£2,761 gross, .4:2.081. rateable. It was against the latter assessment, which was reported to the COM- ruittee yesterday, that the firm now appealed. Mr. Herbert Hatton (Messrs. Robert Davies and Co.), who appeared for Messrs Greenall, Whitley and Co. said they very much appreciated the courteous and reasonable way in which tLe valuer had gore about the business. At the same time be was the servant of the Assessment Committee, and one naturally expected hint to keep the assessment as high as he could, in reason. He (Mr. Havon) hoped to show the COM- mittee that they should bring the assessment down to the lowest possible point that would be permissible. Prior to a re-valuation made last year, the brewery was assessed at 4...2,176 gross, f.2,021-rateable. He , . .

wished to say something by way of comparison beta-yea the premises on which that valuation was made and the creniies as they stood to-day. In the first place the actual brewing plant was less. They had broken up several fermenting vessels and taken two coppers out of their usual use. Against that they had made certain additions. They had to keep pace with the modern practice and therefore motor waggons bad been brought into use and new sheds were built for their accommodation. They bad also covered In the washing yard, which was formerly open, and that necessitated the rebuilding of the cooper's shop. None of the alterations bad in any way increased the badness capacity of the premises. The old valuation was mide after considerable enlargments and additions had been carried cut in anticipation of an 'cores! ed output. That increased output never came to pass and even on the old valuation the plant', as it stood, was in °seers of requirements and remained so to-day. The real question for the committee to con- sider was : What is the letting value of these premises to the tenant? In the Brat place be thought he might put it as an accepted fact that the consump- tion of beer all over the country was less to-day than it was when the old valuation was made. The habits of the people were altering and the consumption of beer had ondocbtedly diminished—probably by at least 10 per cent, in the pact Bre years. Then a new tax of 20e. per 100 barrels was levied under the Finance Act of 1910 and there was also thrown upon the brewer the necessitT of contributing towards the increased license duties on public houses. He was not com- plaining about the increased burden ; it was the enact- , meat of the majority of our governors in the Houses of Parliament and be accepted it. hut whatever their vlew en that question might be, he pot it to them that the common-sense result was that any tenant taking the premises with that increased yearly outlay would undoubtedly give a lower rent. Applviog the principle laid down in the 'Cross and Shuttle " case, the recent legislation had undoubtedly diminished the letting value of the brewery. . . .

The Valuer: Is not this a trade cost It is not on all-fours with an hotel. Surely, it is a trade coat, just as much as if they put an ears price On malt. I don't agree with your argument. Mr. Hatton : As a matter of practical common- sense, the man who takes on the business has to pay an annual out-going which he did not pay before, and I nay he would consequently give less rent. Continuing, be said his main contention wee that the annual value must necessarily be less than it was prior to the Finance Ant of 1910 and he had shown the committee his grounds for contending that the valuation at which the ' brewery stood prior to the re-valuation of last year, was,if anything, too high. lie wee bound to say that the reduction which the con, mitt( e previously gave to .£l,OOO rateable was really only a fair and proper judgment , under the existing conditions. The committee gave roughly a 25 per cent, reduction on the previous rate- ablo value. He found from inquiry that at Burton, the centre of the industry, in face of considerable opposi- tion the brewers obtained a permanent reduction of j from 2.t? 30 per cent, on their rateable assessments.

The Valuer said be had taken into account-71;1;a that the brewery was not at present working to its full capacity. He had had full information and had given so honest and, he hoped, a satiefactory valuation. The committee decided to confirm the figures recom- mended.by the valuer : £2,701 gross ; £2,001 rateable. . . . .

Mr. Hatton : I don't tank- cOu—sie—q-'ilite Lir:lu; justice. The Chairman said the committee ihetight that if they were erring at all, it was hi the appellaine favour.

MARKED "PRIVATE."

Mr. R. Pierpoint and a New Annoyance.

Business anSputlic men are suffering from." a new abuse (says the "Standard.") It seems that a great msny of the letters sent through the post marked "Private," "Confidential," or " Personal " are really nothing of the sort, and men who have a large corres- poodence, complain that the chief advantige they obtain from their secretaries—protection from irrelevant and merely time-wasting correspondence— is beginning to fail them, owing to the tendency which the advertising quack and the begging letter-writer ars displaying to label their envelopes " Private," and to getting their communications past the oumr guard. Mr. Robert Pierpoint, J.P , who seas till 1906 member of Parliament for Warrington, has for some time been setting aside envelopes so marked, with notes as to their contents. An analysis of 117 of these gives the following results : Really private To some extent pi ivate Ordinary Ins-me-tax paper., etc , "on bin Majesty's service " Not private Of the last item 26 contained letters from benevolent monerlenders, and one a sample of cboc late from a firm of cocoa manufacturers. "The effect of this endorsement has been that trivial matter has been forwarded to me abroad," says Mr. Pierpoint. "A pamphlet about someb xly's eye tonic and a prospectus of a hook followed me to France, an application for a subsciiption to some little cricket club went to Jamaica. a request that I would take a ticket for a charity concert, to Italy. Soule years ago a publisher sent me by parcel post a gratis copy of a book on some social or political question, marked " Private." It was forwarded to me abroad at foreignl rates. A body called the 'Employers' Parliamentary Council " nevi to favour me when I was in Parlia- ment with much printed matter, generally in open envelopes, but marked ' Private.' " It should surely be an unwritten law that the words "private," "confidential," or" personal" should oaly appear on correspodence which is really confidential. The endornement on an envelope is a matter of common convenience which, apart altogether from the moral side of the question, it is to the interest of every- body not to abuse, since cantinued abuse will rob it of all value, and men with large correspondence will I have either to waste hours on the irrelevant or surrender their most intimate affairs to a secretary. This abuse of the endorsement on envelopes is only • petty matter, but is eryciptomif c fan imperfect understanding of social obligations. It is worse than criminal . it is vulgar.
